#3002. IBM Stock Is Down About 23% in 2026. Here's Where I Think It Will Be in 2030.

Can a software comeback and an ordinary valuation bring the stock back near its old peak?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 19, 2026 at 9:04PM EST

10 stocks we like better than International Business Machines ›

My best estimate for International Business Machines (IBM -2.04%) is a share price of about $300 when 2030 ends. From roughly $227 as I write, that comes to a rise of about 32%, or about 7% a year before dividends.

I get there by multiplying the non-GAAP (adjusted) earnings per share I think IBM can earn in 2030, about $16.75, by 18 -- a price-to-earnings ratio in line with the stock's own history.

Even $300 is below the June 2 record close of $329.23. Shares today trade around 31% under that high, and they're down about 23% in 2026.

Most of this estimate depends on IBM's software business. For the third quarter, the segment made up around 46% of IBM's $17.1 billion in revenue but about 65% of its segment profit (the profit IBM reports for each business line).

Showing how much the business has bounced back, software revenue grew 9% year over year in constant currency (a measure that removes exchange-rate moves) in the third quarter. This was a big acceleration from 5% in the second quarter. And the second quarter had itself marked a slowdown from 8% in the first quarter and 11% in the fourth quarter of 2025. Better yet, software's segment profit margin improved to 35% from 33% a year before, so the segment is keeping more profit per dollar of sales.

In January, management expected software to grow around 10% this year. On IBM's Oct. 21 earnings call, CFO Jim Kavanaugh said the company now sees around 7% to 8%, with 7% as the base case.

But IBM's annual recurring revenue, a yearly run rate for its recurring software business, rose 8% to $24.9 billion in the third quarter. And Kavanaugh said he expects the growth rate to approach double digits in the fourth quarter.

IBM's adjusted earnings per share reached $11.59 in 2025, up 12% from 2024. So far in 2026, through September, they've risen around 10% to $7.79. Quarterly growth moved from 19% to 5% to 11% -- the same drop and rebound software went through.

If fourth-quarter earnings per share just equal last year's $4.52, IBM would end 2026 at around $12.31.

From there, I assume earnings per share climb around 8% a year, hitting about $16.75 in 2030. That's slower than the last two years, mostly because the rest of IBM grows more slowly than software. Consulting, its second-biggest segment, grew just 2% at constant currency in the third quarter. Also, IBM's diluted share count ticked up in the last year, to around 954 million, so buybacks aren't doing much for earnings per share.

When 2022 ended, IBM stock traded at around 15 times earnings, using that year's adjusted earnings per share. The ratio climbed to about 17 times earnings by the close of 2023, then to 21 times earnings a year later and 26 times earnings by the end of 2025.

At the June record, shares cost around 28 times adjusted earnings for the previous four quarters.

Now IBM stock goes for about 18 times the adjusted earnings it posted in the last year, and about 17 times analysts' average earnings forecast for 2027. So shares arguably sit toward the low end of their recent range already.

I pick 18 times earnings for 2030 -- close to today's price-to-earnings ratio and under the average of the four year-ends, about 20 times earnings. Applied to about $16.75 in earnings per share, this gives a stock price near $300.

Of course, software might move the result either way. If it gets back to the 10% growth IBM expected in January and earnings per share gain about 10% a year, the stock could hit about $325 at the same valuation.

But if software settles near the second quarter's 5% rate and investors pay 15 times earnings, like they did at the end of 2022, shares might finish 2030 about where they are now.

IBM, which has paid dividends every year since 1916, also pays $1.69 a share each quarter, a yield of around 3% at today's price. Add it to about 7% a year from price gains, and my estimate suggests a total return of about 10% a year through 2030.

That's not a stellar return, but I think it's a good one for a company whose most profitable segment is growing faster again. I'd be comfortable buying IBM shares near today's price.

#2998. Tilray Has More Cash Than Debt and 71% Growth in Europe. Is This Cannabis Stock a Buy?

Tilray has dramatically reshaped its business, but there's still one missing piece that will keep many investors away.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 19, 2026 at 9:26PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Tilray Brands ›

For more aggressive growth investors, Tilray Brands (TLRY -8.00%) appears to be moving in a positive direction as a business. It may be worth buying if you have a long time horizon. However, more conservative investors will probably want to remain on the sidelines because the company still hasn't achieved one important goal: sustainable profitability. Here's why this financially strong cannabis seller is worth buying for some investors, but not for others.

When Tilray first went public, its goal was to take advantage of the increasing legal access to cannabis. There was significant excitement in the market around the marijuana sector at the time, and the stock rose sharply alongside the rest of the sector. But the reality didn't live up to Wall Street's expectations. Competition was intense, the legalization trend was slow, and illicit marijuana sales remained robust. Tilray and the rest of the sector fell sharply.

Tilray decided to move in a different direction. The goal now is to be a "global lifestyle and consumer packaged goods company leading at the nexus of the beverage, cannabis, and wellness industries." Effectively, the company is looking to operate more like a consumer staples company than a pure-play cannabis business. The biggest shift was the acquisition of alcoholic beverage brands.

In the first quarter of fiscal 2027, revenues from Tilray's alcoholic beverages stood at $101.5 million, while its cannabis revenues totaled $56.1 million. You could easily argue that Tilray is now more of an alcohol company than a marijuana company. However, the bigger story is the diversification that the company has created. That should attract growth investors who want exposure to cannabis, but who don't want to own a pure-play cannabis company.

The truth is, marijuana is a growing industry. The problem is that companies have had a hard time translating that growth into profits. Tilray is looking to resolve that problem by diversifying into new areas that have a more stable history, notably craft brewing. That creates a foundation on which it can continue to build its marijuana operations.

That foundation, meanwhile, is increasingly strong. Notably, it ended the first fiscal quarter of 2027 with a net cash position. That requires a little parsing, because it means the company's cash balance of roughly $215 million exceeds its long-term debt of $118 million. That's not quite as good as a debt-free balance sheet, but Tilray has a very strong financial position right at the moment.

Meanwhile, the company's growth in Europe has been a strong point, further adding to its diversification. In the first quarter, European revenues increased by 71%. From a high-level perspective, Tilray is a much stronger business today than it was not that long ago. If you are a growth investor with a long-term focus, you may want to take a closer look. It could be a good way to add marijuana exposure without having to own a pure-play.

While there is a lot to like about Tilray and the direction the business is moving, there's one important issue that remains unresolved. Tilray continues to bleed red ink on the bottom line. That single fact should probably keep more conservative investors on the sidelines. The direction the business is heading looks good, but until there are sustainable profits, an investment here remains a risky one. Buying it requires believing that Tilray's consumer-staples shift will eventually lead to a profitable and growing business. Only a more aggressive growth investor will likely be willing to make that bet.

#2996. Nvidia Just Raised Its Supply Commitments to $390 Billion, Mostly for Memory. Micron Is the Memory Stock I'd Buy.

If the AI chip giant is locking in memory for years, why is the memory maker's stock off around 42% from its peak?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 19, 2026 at 11:07PM EST

When Nvidia (NVDA -3.30%) reported on Nov. 17, its fiscal third-quarter revenue had more than doubled from the year before, to a record $115.8 billion. But for memory investors, the more telling number was how much the artificial intelligence (AI) chip leader has agreed to pay its suppliers.

Nvidia's supply commitments rose to $390 billion, up from $279 billion a quarter earlier. And the company said the increase was "primarily related to the multi-year procurement of memory supply."

That's good news for Micron Technology (MU -2.10%), one of the three big makers of the memory chips Nvidia buys. But Micron stock is around $702 as I write, about 42% under the record $1,213.56 it closed at on June 25.

I think the market's underrating what Nvidia's report says about Micron's business.

Showing how fast its buying has picked up, Nvidia's supply commitments have more than tripled over two quarters. They were at $119 billion as of April 26, climbed to $279 billion by July 26, and hit $390 billion by Oct. 25, the end of its fiscal third quarter. In both of the last two quarters, the company linked most of the rise to memory. What's more, the money's coming due sooner. Of the $390 billion, $203 billion falls in Nvidia's fiscal 2028, which ends in January 2028. Three months earlier, that year's figure was $87 billion.

In short, Nvidia is locking in memory long before it needs the chips, and much of this spending will probably land within about a year.

Nvidia doesn't say how it splits the spending between its memory suppliers. SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics sell Nvidia high-bandwidth memory (HBM), too. (HBM is the stacked memory that sits next to AI processors.)

Micron doesn't name its largest buyers, either. However, no single customer made up 10% or more of its revenue for its fiscal 2026, which ended Sept. 3, down from one customer at 17% the year before.

So Micron's direct sales to Nvidia, whatever they are, were under a tenth of its revenue last year.

What Micron does say is encouraging. It's working with Nvidia on what it calls the industry's first custom HBM4E chip, designed for Nvidia's next generation of graphics processing units.

And its cloud memory business unit, which sells HBM to all data center customers, keeps growing. It pulled in $16.3 billion of revenue in the three months ended Sept. 3, up 18% from the previous quarter and more than triple the $4.5 billion of a year before, with an 83% gross margin. On its fiscal fourth-quarter call, Micron said it had lifted its HBM prices significantly for calendar 2027.

Nvidia's commitments look like the buyer's side of a change Micron is already building its business around.

Micron has now signed 26 multiyear strategic customer agreements, up from 16 a quarter earlier. All are take-or-pay deals, so customers commit to buying fixed volumes and can lose deposits if they fall short.

Customers have committed $32 billion under the deals, mainly as cash deposits, compared to the $22 billion Micron forecast a quarter before.

Micron also puts its remaining performance obligations (contracted sales it hasn't recognized yet) at around $150 billion, counting just committed volumes at minimum prices. "[E]ven at floor prices, we expect margins meaningfully above any prior cycle peak margins," chief financial officer Mark Murphy said in Micron's prepared remarks for the fiscal fourth quarter.

But the stock trades like the boom might end soon. At around $702, Micron's price-to-earnings ratio is about 4 based on analysts' estimates for fiscal 2027 (which ends next September), and only about 9 on fiscal 2026's actual earnings of $74.33 per share. A price like that suggests investors expect profits to dive before long.

Sure, memory prices have crashed before, and Micron plans to spend more on new plant space, which might loosen supply in late 2028 and beyond. But a growing share of its sales now comes with committed volumes, deposits, and price floors, protections Micron didn't have in past memory busts.

Of the three companies selling Nvidia its memory, Micron is the one I'd buy. It's working with Nvidia on custom HBM, and unlike Samsung, it's a pure memory and storage business. At around 4 times expected earnings, the stock looks cheap to me even if Nvidia's money is split three ways.

#2994. Julius Randle on Nets' 'embarrassing' blowout loss to Bulls: 'One of the worst games I've ever been a part of'

The Brooklyn Nets wore red, white and blue alternate uniforms on Wednesday night, throwing it back to the franchise's ABA heyday when they welcomed the Chicago Bulls to Barclays Center. The evening featured an appearance by 10 members of the teams that won ABA championships in 1974 and 1976 — including the legendary "Dr. J," Julius Erving.

"I fully anticipated the Nets to be so fired up having us here, they would blow Chicago out," the Hall of Famer said during an appearance on the YES Network broadcast midway through the second quarter. "But I'm looking at 62-39, so … I was wrong."

The Nets scored Wednesday's first basket, with young guard Nolan Traoré getting a runout dunk after Egor Dëmin picked off a Josh Giddey pass. A minute later, Bulls guard Norman Powell knocked down a 3-pointer to put Chicago on top … and, well, that was all she wrote for Brooklyn.

The Nets never again led, or even tied, the visitors, trailing by as many as 37 points in a truly dismal performance that settled as a 137-107 blowout loss. At home. At the hands of what had been a three-win team, with the NBA's fifth-worst offense and absolute worst defense … and without exciting lottery pick Caleb Wilson.

It was a wretched outing — one that saw the Nets commit 20 turnovers leading to 27 Chicago points, and allow 15 first-half 3-pointers and 76 first-half points, the third straight game in which they conceded 70-plus before intermission. Brooklyn gave up 62 total points in the paint, and sat idly by as eight different Bulls scored in double figures.

In the midst of the depressing showing, courtside cameras caught Brooklyn forward Julius Randle lighting into his teammates on the Nets bench:

After finishing the night with 17 points, four assists and no rebounds in 27 minutes of work in the lopsided loss, Randle — who's soldiered through a 17-win season with the Lakers, the post-Anthony Davis doldrums in New Orleans, and the 21-win end of the David Fizdale era in New York — sounded a similarly dour note about the state of affairs for the 3-11 Nets in his postgame press conference.

"That was embarrassing," Randle said, according to Brian Lewis of the New York Post. "Been in the league for 13 years, and that's one of the worst games I've ever been a part of. And not because of the score … but it's how we did it. That wasn't cool. Nothing about it was cool. We've got to find a way to figure out the root of it or whatever, but that's just unacceptable. It's just a pride, an effort, the competitive spirit — all of that, it was nonexistent."

The Nets entered Wednesday intending to celebrate the high-water mark in franchise history, but wound up closer to a historic nadir. According to Erik Slater of Clutch Points, the last two losses "mark the first time they've given up 135 points in back-to-back games since April 1 [and] 2, 1969."

That's a far cry from where the Nets hoped to be after trading this summer for Randle, a three-time All-Star and two-time All-NBA selection who'd starred for the cross-town New York Knicks before helping the Minnesota Timberwolves make a pair of playoff runs, to pair with incumbent sweet-shooting forward Michael Porter Jr. in the frontcourt, in hopes of restoring a franchise that had the third-worst record in the NBA over the preceding three seasons to respectability.

But despite the presence of the 20-point-scoring veteran forwards and some exciting flashes from young guards Traoré, Dëmin and rookie Mikel Brown Jr., Brooklyn has remained mired in the muck, ranking among the league's worst teams on both ends of the court and looking like a team without much of a sense of who it wants to be, how it wants to play, and what its path to success might look like.

"Very disappointed, just starting with effort," head coach Jordi Fernández said after the game, according to C.J. Holmes of the New York Daily News. "We were not a competitive group. We were not a connected group. We were not a selfless group. I saw selfishness. I saw pointing fingers, and I saw guys that didn't always play hard."

That's not the brand of ball that Fernández has preached since arriving in Brooklyn in 2024. The team's desultory effort, though, suggests that maybe what he's preaching isn't quite getting across.

"I can try to say it in Spanish, because in English, they don't understand," Fernández quipped after the game. "So I'll keep trying and find whatever language works. But yeah, our effort was not good."

Julius Randle didn't mince words when describing the state of affairs for his 3-11 Nets. (Caleb Bowlin/Getty Images)

If the coach's message is falling on deaf ears, then it's up to the players to stem the tide and turn things around. Randle appeared to be searching for a way to do so with his impassioned entreaty on the bench; with another game coming against the Orlando Magic on Friday, he says he and his teammates have to look within for the answers they're seeking.

"I think when you win, you find ways to win. When you lose, you find excuses. Seems like a lot of excuses," Randle said, according to Collin Helwig of NetsDaily. "So, you know, for me, I've got to go back into work. I've got to go back into the film. I've got to figure out — I've got to look myself in the mirror tonight, too, like everybody else, and figure out what I can do better. Not point the finger or anything. Like, I got to figure out as a man [...] that has played this game since a kid and love it, and has given a lot to me, I got to figure out how I can help the team and be better for the team. So, you know, I put it on me."

#2993. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been volatile as investors assess high bond yields and continued tensions between the U.S. and Iran. There are concerns about the impact of uncertainty in the Middle East on oil prices and consequently on inflation.

Investors who prefer to look beyond short-term noise and buy stocks with attractive long-term growth potential can follow the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. The ratings of these experts are backed by in-depth research of a company's financials and thorough analysis of the factors affecting its business performance.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Semiconductor giant Nvidia (NVDA) reported stellar fiscal second-quarter results and reassured investors about continued strength in AI-driven demand for its chips. Moreover, Nvidia's FY28 revenue growth outlook of 70% was well above the Street's expectations.

Reacting to the Q2 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on NVDA and increased his price target to $300 from $288. "This is our Top Pick in the semis group, with a compelling product cycle, exceptional growth and valuation below peers," said the 5-star analyst.

Nvidia delivered impressive quarterly results despite supply constraints, Moore said, but he believes that the FY28 revenue and gross margin commentary mattered more, with a favorable outlook for both measures.

Moore said Nvidia's 70% revenue growth guidance for FY28 was above his own estimate of 52% and the consensus of about 40%. The top-line growth estimate was a "remarkable figure" given supply issues and Moore said he expects Nvidia to continue to address bottlenecks to deliver rapid growth.

Among other takeaways, the analyst said performance metrics for Vera Rubin chips were strong, with Nvidia emphasizing a 30x higher throughput per megawatt and 35x lower token cost compared with Grace Blackwell Ultra.

Moore ranks No. 159 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 24.60%. See Nvidia Statistics on TipRanks.

Ride-hailing and delivery platform Uber Technologies (UBER) was recommended by BMO Capital analyst Brian Pitz, who discussed the company's opportunity in the autonomous vehicle (AV) market in a recent research note that reiterated a buy rating and price target of $119.

The 5-star analyst highlighted Uber's transition into a diversified marketplace, with investors now focusing on its AV growth prospects. The key question is how much value can Uber generate as AVs become commercialized, Pitz asked.

"We continue to believe that Uber's AV strategy is evolving and will become a significant driver of revenue and profitability," the analyst said.

Importantly, Pitz believes that Uber can establish itself as a preferred mobility platform for AV makers, supported by its growing AV infrastructure capabilities and an expanding partner base. This would enable Uber to win a significant share of value as the AV market is commercialized.

Additionally, Pitz noted Uber's enhanced position in the AV value chain, with the company shifting from serving as a distribution channel for robotaxis to becoming a broader platform for AV mobility. The analyst discussed Uber's efforts to capture growth opportunities in the AV market, including higher capital spending, expansion into new markets and building partnerships beyond Alphabet's Waymo.

Pitz ranks No. 574 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of about 12.20%. See Uber Technologies Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the second quarter of FY27. But the company's increased FY28 guidance fell short of investors' high expectations.

In reaction to the Q2 FY27 results, KeyBanc analyst John Vinh reaffirmed a buy rating on Marvell Technology, with a price target of $400. The analyst noted that Marvell's results and outlook were driven by strength in its data center business. The Q2 FY27 data center business grew 46% year-over-year, to $2.17 billion, far above KeyBanc's estimate of $2.09 billion.

Management "highlighted accelerating AI networking demand across scale-out, scale-across and scale-up architectures, supported by strong 800G optical DSP demand, rapid 1.6T ramp, broader 51.2T switch deployments and higher TIA/driver demand," Vinh said.

Consequently, Marvell raised its data center revenue guidance, now expecting 60% growth in FY27 and more than 60% in FY28.

Regarding the recently announced $120 billion warrant arrangement with Google, Vinh said management argued that programs tied to the warrant are already priced into the custom outlook through FY28. Meanwhile, new programs and those in execution are expected to drive significant upside to the previous FY29 custom XPU revenue estimate of over $10 billion, Vinh said, citing management.

Vinh ranks No. 123 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 28.50%. See Marvell Technology Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2992. AI Token Prices Are Tumbling. Is It a Red Flag for Anthropic Stock?

Token prices are falling just as Anthropic goes public. Is it a warning sign for the IPO stock?

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 20, 2026 at 12:30AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Anthropic (ANT +17.07%) just finished its IPO day with the AI lab jumping 17% in its blockbuster debut.

Anthropic's debut exposes stock market investors to AI labs for the first time, and Anthropic may have a broader range of potential outcomes than any stock before it. The company has identified a market opportunity valued at $40 trillion, and its technology is so powerful that CEO Dario Amodei thinks it could cure diseases like cancer and Alzheimer's within a decade. In contrast, a researcher for the company said there's more than a 10% chance that it "could kill all humans."

Likewise, Anthropic bulls, including Goldman Sachs, projected $440 billion in revenue by 2029, and Morningstar's bull case put its share price at $188.

Anthropic bears, on the other hand, have argued that the company doesn't have a sustainable competitive advantage and faces too much competition from cheaper models and the threat of distillation.

Among the top critiques is that token prices are falling sharply, as Bloomberg's chief economist argues below.

In today’s piece @TheTerminal, I write about why Anthropic IPO may mark the end of the AI trade. https://t.co/BhCWhrbIbM Two charts summarized it the argument: 1) Its effective price per million token has come down sharply of late (its s-1 cutoff date gives financial statements up to 9/30) 2) yet its token usage share didn’t pick up, and even edged down That reveals the kind of market structure that it operates in. It seems to be an oligopolist who does not have durable pricing power. Growth has to rely on price cuts to drive volume. That is a tough business.

The usage-weighted Silicon Data LLM Token Expenditure Index, which tracks prices per million tokens, fell more than 40% between early June 30 and early October, and prices have continued to fall since then.

Anthropic has even cut its prices by 20% on its newest frontier model, Fable 5.2.

Token prices are falling for a number of reasons. First, competition from cheaper models is pushing prices across the industry lower. Second, customers are shifting their consumption habits and using cheaper models for more routine tasks, and third, costs to generate tokens are falling due to more efficient computing usage, allowing Anthropic to lower prices without necessarily losing profit.

The consequences of lower token prices aren't fully clear. Superficially, lower prices would seem to be bad, as it would lower Anthropic's revenue, but lower prices can also drive increased usage, a relationship known as the Jevons paradox, which is a benefit over the long term.

Anthropic's strong growth through the third quarter indicates that the company can grow even while token prices are falling, as it reported 47% sequential growth in the third quarter. Additionally, the company has managed to scale revenue faster than its computing costs, resulting in an adjusted operating profit.

Still, falling token prices could put pressure on Anthropic, especially if rising competition and usage shifts are driving them.

CEO Dario Amodei has called for a crackdown on industrial-scale distillation operations, referring to a method of training AI models that uses existing models, rather than training on data from scratch. Distillation is comparable to counterfeiting in other industries. It lowers prices, but does so at the expense of undercutting the investment and research and development that market leaders have made, disincentivizing it in the long run. Amodei also sees it as a national security threat, as distillation allows China to build much better models than it normally would be able to.

In technology, prices often fall over time due to competition and as the technology evolves and gets more efficient.

Amazon invented cloud computing and remains the market leader, even though the company has cut costs hundreds of times due to economies of scale, competitive pressure, and incentivizing customers to stay on its platform. AWS continues to grow and has become one of the most valuable businesses in the world, even on a stand-alone basis.

Anthropic has a number of similarities to AWS as a leader in a new technology with a massive addressable market ahead of it, and pricing is just one part of the equation.

Demand for artificial intelligence could be nearly limitless, and investors shouldn't lose sight of that. While token prices are worth monitoring, if Anthropic can continue to grow profits, falling token prices aren't the red flag that bears seem to think they are.

#2988. This Unstoppable ETF Is Up by Double Digits in 2026. Here's Where I Think It's Headed in 2027.

Chip stocks have undergone a correction over the past several weeks.

By Dave Kovaleski – Nov 20, 2026 at 1:10AM EST

10 stocks we like better than iShares Trust - iShares Semiconductor ETF ›

One of the hottest exchange-traded funds (ETFs) this year has been the iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX -2.75%). This ETF, which tracks an index that features the 30 largest U.S. semiconductor stocks, has returned an impressive 37% this year and 49% over the past 12 months.

But since the recent peak of $196 per share on Oct. 6, the day the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -2.25%) reached an all-time high, the ETF has been in a downward spiral, with the price dropping about 30% to $138 per share at this writing.

A combination of factors caused the meltdown, including overvalued large caps, concerns about overspending on artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure, and negative sentiment toward building more data centers — particularly as Democrats swept into Congress.

Also, the Nvidia (NVDA -3.30%) scandal and the resignation of its longtime CEO Jensen Huang pulled down semiconductor and technology stocks pretty much across the board.

So, it has been a rough couple of months for semiconductor stocks, but here's why I think the iShares Semiconductor ETF will have another strong year in 2027.

The sharp sell-off of semiconductor stocks should serve as a reset of sorts. The correction was overdue because many top chip stocks had become overvalued amid rapid growth over the past three to four years.

This reset should bring their valuations down to more attractive, sustainable levels, potentially sparking a bounce-back rally in 2027.

A slowdown in data center development is certainly on the table, if not likely, given that it was a major issue in the recent election campaigns. That may have a longer-term impact, and it may not -- only time will tell. There just may be more restrictions and parameters on where data centers are located and how they operate.

But it shouldn't have a major effect on chip stocks in 2027. Hyperscalers have a huge amount of backlog of contracts on the books waiting to be executed, and many chipmakers are already sold out of AI chips for 2027 and into 2028.

Also, the supercycle created by the supply-demand imbalance among memory and storage stocks remains. Supply is not expected to catch up to demand for at least a few years, and that gives chipmakers huge pricing power. A potential slowdown in building AI data centers could help ease the supply-demand imbalance somewhat, but likely down the road beyond 2027.

So, the correction in chip stocks might be painful right now, but I think it makes for a more attractive buying opportunity for the iShares Semiconductor ETF heading into 2027.

#2987. Jayson Tatum owns late technical that sealed Boston's loss to Denver

To be clear: Jayson Tatum's technical foul with 6.4 seconds left in regulation wasn't the reason Boston lost to Denver on Wednesday night. The Nuggets dominated the second half, and when they doubled Tatum, the Celtics' offense struggled to be efficient, especially when their 3-point shooting went cold.

But after a Jamal Murray bucket that put the Nuggets up 104-101 with 6.4 seconds left, Tatum was hit with a controversial technical for throwing the ball toward the official John Goble, who wasn't looking at him, rather than handing it to baseline official, Eric Dalen, who was near him. Tatum and the Celtics were frustrated about a no-call on a screen to get Murray open for the shot and Dalen saw Tatum's toss as throwing the ball at an official in frustration. Tatum said postgame that was not the case.

"I told [Dalen], I wasn't mad. Wasn't a foul on me, we just called a timeout," Tatum said. "John Goble called a timeout and pointed at our bench. Thought he was looking this way, but yeah, he called a tech. For me, just find the female ref that was there, or the other ref, and whoever was probably closest, I should have just gave them the ball."

Murray sank the technical free throw, making it a four point game, and that was essentially the ballgame. The Heat could not just tie it in one possession.

After the game, coach Joe Mazzulla said Tatum has to be better as the team leader.

"We can't put ourselves in that position to even give the refs an opportunity to interpret it a certain type of way," Mazzulla said from the podium. "Gotta be better."

Tatum dropped 20 on the Nuggets in the first half. However, Denver focused more on doubling him in the second half, and he scored just four points on 2-of-6 shooting with five turnovers. Mazzulla said Tatum needs to be better under pressure than he was in the second half. Boston also needs to find a way to score more efficiently when defenses focus on him, because that is what will happen in the playoffs. There is a long time to go between now and the start of the postseason, but this is when coaches and teams need to build the good habits and patterns they will need in the NBA's second season.

#2984. Cameron Boozer grabs top spot in NBA rookie rankings as Grizzlies surge

The NBA Rookie of the Year race is beginning to take shape after the first month of the 2026-27 season, though no player has managed to separate himself from the pack. The top two picks in this year's draft remain on the periphery of the conversation, while a trio of less-heralded contenders has emerged at the front.

Yaxel Lendeborg of the Golden State Warriors, Memphis Grizzlies forward Cameron Boozer and Sacramento Kings guard Darius Acuff, Jr. have been the early standouts, with Lendeborg and Boozer both playing key roles for winning teams. AJ Dybantsa and Darryn Peterson lurk closely behind after slower starts to the campaign, and there's a collection of other rookies contributing in significant ways with many teams emphasizing depth to combat the NBA's injury issues.

Indeed, as of Nov. 19, there are 20 rookies averaging at least 15 minutes per game.

Here's how the top NBA rookies stack up after the first month of the 2026-27 regular season.

NBA POWER RANKINGS: Giannis, Heat surge; Warriors plunge without Curry

Stats as of games played on Wednesday, Nov. 18. Rookie of the year odds as of 11:50 a.m. ET on Nov. 19, courtesy of BetMGM. Click here for last week's rankings.

Stats: 16.3 points, 6.6 rebounds, 2.3 assists

Dybantsa emerged from a three-game slump to produce the two best all-around performances of his young NBA career, including his first double-double (23 points, 12 rebounds) in a win over the Toronto Raptors on Wednesday that snapped an eight-game losing streak for the Wizards. He continues to struggle shooting from outside and is hitting just 21.3% of his 3-pointers as of Nov. 19. When he scored a career-best 28 points in a Nov. 13 loss to the Houston Rockets, he became the second-youngest player in NBA history behind only Anthony Edwards to record five-straight games with 20 or more points. After Utah's stunning win over the Lakers on Wednesday, Peterson had 34 3-pointers and 29 stocks (steals + blocks) in his first 15 NBA games. The only other player to do that through his first 15 career games is Allen Iverson, according to the Jazz.

Stats: 26.2 points, 3.6 rebounds, 7 assists

The Kings haven't translated Acuff's prolific scoring into wins yet, but he keeps scoring at rates rarely seen by an NBA rookie. He had his fifth game with 30 or more points this season on Wednesday when scored 36 points on 50% shooting in a blowout loss to the Phoenix Suns. One of the few bright spots in another miserable Kings' season is still on pace to average the most points by an NBA rookie since Michael Jordan.

Stats: 20.3 points, 7.3 rebounds, 3.5 assists

Lendeborg endured the first rough patch of his otherwise stellar rookie season this past week with defenses keying on him while Stephen Curry remains sidelined by injury. Lendeborg shot less than 30% from the field, including 2-for-14 from 3-point range, as Golden State suffered three consecutive blowout losses. He nevertheless continues to lead all rookies in player efficiency rating while carrying a star-level offensive burden for a veteran-laden playoff contender. Few first-year players are asked to shoulder that kind of responsibility.

Stats: 16.6 points, 9.4 rebounds, 5.3 assists

Boozer vaults to No. 1 in the rookie rankings for the first time season after leading the surprising Grizzlies to four-straight wins, including a Nov. 16 win over the Washington Wizards in which he set career-highs with 29 points and seven 3-pointers matched up against Anthony Davis. Boozer ranks among the top three rookies in rebounds, assists, NET rating, plus/minus and player efficiency rating thus far, and his shooting numbers are beginning to normalize after a slow start from the field. No player in this rookie class has impacted winning more than Boozer through the season's first month.

#2981. Top Wall Street analysts recommend these 3 dividend stocks for higher returns

Dividend-paying stocks can enhance portfolio returns by providing a steady income stream to investors and the potential for capital appreciation. Several investors seek exposure to dividend stocks in a volatile backdrop to ensure stable income.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, backed by strong financials and cash flows to support consistent dividend payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Energy Transfer (ET) is a diversified midstream energy company with about 140,000 miles of pipelines and associated energy infrastructure in 44 states. The company made a quarterly cash distribution of 34 cents per common unit for the second quarter of 2026. At an annualized payment of $1.36 per common unit, ET offers a yield of 6.3%.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Jeremy Tonet reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer stock and slightly raised his price target to $25 from $24. The analyst noted that the company delivered a strong Q2 beat across all key metrics.

Tonet highlighted that Energy Transfer raised its 2026 adjusted EBITDA guidance to $18.8 billion-$19.1 billion from $18.2 billion-$18.6 billion, reflecting robust base results and cost optimization. The 5-star analyst added that the company "tightened" its capex to $5.6 billion–$5.9 billion and expects capex above $5 billion annually through 2029, given solid opportunities.

About the Hugh Brinson pipeline, Tonet noted Phase 1 (1.5 bcfd) reaching full capacity by September 1 and Phase II capacity (0.7 bcfd) going online by Q1 2027. The analyst also noted completion of a 14-mile Hugh Brinson lateral in Abilene, Texas, and final stages of talks for an additional 250 mmcfd of Oklahoma power plant demand.

"All in, we see ET well positioned to continue capitalizing on organic growth opportunities across the value chain," said Tonet.

Tonet ranks No. 922 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 9%. See Energy Transfer Financials on TipRanks.

This week's second dividend pick is independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR). The company declared a base dividend of $0.16 per share for the third quarter, payable on September 30, 2026. At an annualized dividend of $0.64 per share, PR offers a yield of about 2.7%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Permian Resources stock and increased his price target to $27 from $22, citing an improved outlook on oil production growth and operational efficiencies.

The 5-star analyst noted that while PR stock has outperformed its peers so far in 2026, he sees further upside. Mehta is confident about the company's operational improvements and ability to deliver incremental free cash flow per share through the strategic ground game program. He added that the program has completed about $1.05 billion in bolt-on deals this year (as of August 5).

"We see the continued success of PR's ground game as constructive toward future inventory replenishment and increasing working interest over time," said Mehta.

The analyst also emphasized Permian Resources' solid track record of generating growth in free cash flow (FCF) per share. Mehta expects the company's free cash flow per share to grow at a 20% CAGR (compound annual growth rate) from 2025–2028.

Mehta ranks No. 401 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.8%. See Permian Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Sempra Energy (SRE), a utility holding company. Earlier this month, the company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.6575 per share, payable on October 15. At an annualized dividend of $2.63 per share, SRE stock offers a yield of about 3.1%.

Recently, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith upgraded SRE stock to Buy from Hold but slightly lowered the price target to $97 from $101. The 5-star analyst noted that Sempra stock is trading at a price/earnings multiple that is 14% below its electric peers due to concerns about Texas transmission capital expenditure and the failure of California legislation.

Smith noted that the derating of weaker California peers PG&E (PCG) and Edison International (EIX) is weighing on the sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation but is becoming less relevant given their materially higher fire risk compared to Sempra Energy.

Furthermore, Smith expects Sempra Energy's Texas capex plan to stay in place despite the opposition to transmission and data centers, though delays to 765-kV projects are likely. While a full recovery for SRE stock will need a clearer outcome from the Texas legislative session, Smith believes that the stock may already be an early opportunity for patient investors.

"We are early on the upgrade but see limited downside at current levels as the market is pricing in little transmission upside," said Smith.

Smith ranks No. 945 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.2%. See Sempra ETF Exposure on TipRanks.

#2978. Takeaways from Sixers-Magic: The clutch kings do it again

Things weren't looking so hot for your Sixers Wednesday night.

Jaylen Brown tweaked his hamstring late in the win over Brooklyn Monday and had been ruled out Tuesday ahead of Wednesday's game. Dean Wade also had a hamstring issue, popping up on the injury report as questionable in the afternoon before being ruled out in the evening. Then, Tyrese Maxey was listed as questionable after warming up in Orlando. He was also ruled out right before tip-off.

Then, the Sixers found themselves down 17 in the first quarter as the Magic were red-hot to start the game. It felt like maybe it just wasn't the Sixers' night. These things happen over the course of an 82-game season.

But this isn't the same old Sixers. Led by VJ Edgecombe, and with contributions up and down the lineup, they moved to 11-0 in clutch games, securing a 121-116 win. They're 13-3 and sit alone atop the East.

Even with LeBron James and Joel Embiid available, it was likely going to take a great night from Edgecombe for the Sixers to win.

It was another night where he was everywhere and stuffed the stat sheet with 24 points (13 in the fourth), eight rebounds (three offensive), six assists and two steals. He was efficient, going 8-of-15 from the floor and 4-of-9 from three.

But the box score doesn't come close to telling the story. He made clutch shots, timely passes, defensive plays and seemed to be around the ball all night, especially late in the game.

The last five minutes of this one showed you all Edgecombe has to offer. Around the five-minute mark, he got downhill off an Embiid screen and found Jabari Walker in the corner for three, giving the Sixers their first lead since the opening minutes of the game. After the teams were jostling back and forth, Orlando went up by one on a Jalen Suggs' three. On the next possession, James passed off a grenade to Edgecombe, who coolly buried a deep triple with a hand in his face.

On the next possession, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope stood up Franz Wagner, who had his way for most of the night (Edgecombe also had a couple terrific defensive reps on Wagner late). Then, Edgecombe fought for the rebound, knocking it off Desmond Bane out of bounds. The best part is Edgecombe seemed more fired up about the hustle play than the three-ball.

It's to a point where we can't call Edgecombe a "future star" or "star-in-the-making." Screw the qualifiers — this kid is a star right now.

With Maxey, Brown and Edgecombe able to do a lot of heavy lifting, the Sixers aren't as dependent on James and Embiid to be The Guy on a nightly basis — but it's a nice luxury to have two former league MVPs who can scale up roles when needed.

After posting a season-high 37 points against the Nets Monday, Embiid wasn't his most efficient offensively. Still, he had moments where he controlled the game, getting to the line 14 times and throwing off the Magic's rhythm. He finished with a team-high 26 points on 7-of-18 from the field.

But his biggest contributions were on the other end of the floor. The first quarter was ugly. The Sixers' defense was a mess and Orlando was making everything. From the second quarter on, that changed dramatically, with Embiid being a big part of it. He came up with two impressive blocks on Wendell Carter, Jr. to close the first half. With three minutes left and the Sixers clinging to a two-point lead, Embiid came away with an impressive block on Anthony Black, an infamous Sixers killer.

Embiid started the season protecting the rim at a high level. As his plantar fasciitis flared up and he was playing big minutes, that started to wane. Wednesday night was an example of what he can do when he's feeling good and engaged on that end. Of course, he isn't what he once was physically, but this is a guy who was a Defensive Player of the Year finalist. The instincts are still there.

As for LeBron, it's obvious he needs to pick his spots at 41 years old, but the moments that he does are still special. He took the game over the second quarter, posting nine points as the Sixers chipped away at Orlando's huge lead.

Then in the fourth, James came up with multiple big plays. He finished a layup off an Embiid pass — the big fella's seventh assist of the night — from a great Nick Nurse play design(!). After Edgecombe's big three and with the Sixers holding a two-point lead, James was left alone from 20-feet after a screen from Edgecombe. The NBA's all-time leading scorer decided to pull up, burying a jumper to make it a two-possession game. After Suggs split free throws on the other end, James set up Anfernee Simons for the dagger.

Will the Sixers win every clutch game this season? Of course not. Is there a little luck involved? Sure.

But the Sixers' luck feels like it changed in late July when The King chose Philly.

That trio shined, but make no mistake, this was a team effort.

Simons and KCP, starting in place of Maxey and Brown, both played their roles to perfection.

Simons, a Florida native named after Magic legend Anfernee "Penny" Hardaway, dropped 18 points, going 7-of-12 from the field and 3-of-7 from three, including the triple that sealed the game. A positive development in recent games is Simons is starting to make more twos. He's more than capable. He's not just a shooter, he's a bucket-getter.

KCP continues to look like the guy who helped the Lakers and Nuggets win championships. He's probably been the unsung hero for the Sixers' great start. He's been available for every game this season and has made an impact almost every night. He's shooting 44% from three (likely not sustainable, though he's shot over 40% from deep in three different seasons), playing outstanding defense and doing all the little things. He did a great job on Wagner down the stretch and also pulled down eight rebounds on the night. He was a team-high +10.

And let's not forget about Tacko Fall. Nurse let the 7-foot-6 center get two stints in this one and Fall was impactful in both. In a little over 10 minutes, Fall pulled down three rebounds and came away with three blocks. He also got his first bucket off a nice pass from James. The most surprising stat of the night: Fall was a +7 while Embiid was a -1. What a time to be alive.

Walker continues to be a solid contributor, nailing that big three off the Edgecombe pass. Labaron Philon Jr. came off the bench and scored 10 points and recorded two assists in 17 minutes. There's an argument to be made for the rookie to play more minutes to spell Maxey and Edgecombe. Nurse even got Justin Edwards and Caleb Love some run.

They're 11-0 in clutch games. They're 7-1 on the road, their best start away from home since 1982-83. They're 13-3 overall, their best start since 2000-01. What happened in those seasons again?

Again, you don't love saying it so early in the season, but something about this team feels special.

#2977. Why didn't the Colts trade Jonathan Taylor? RB spared in Indy's fire sale

The Indianapolis Colts were the NFL's biggest sellers ahead of the 2026 NFL trade deadline. They offloaded numerous veteran players, including wide receivers Keenan Allen and Josh Downs, defensive lineman DeForest Buckner and long-time, stalwart left guard Quenton Nelson.

But even despite their activity level, the Colts didn't trade their biggest chip. Running back Jonathan Taylor remained in Indianapolis even after being the subject of persistent trade rumors leading up to the deadline.

Taylor has long been a star running back since being selected in the second round of the 2020 NFL Draft. Still, many thought the Colts might cash in and unload the 27-year-old in a blockbuster deal to continue to add to their coffers of draft assets ahead of what figures to be a challenging rebuild.

So, why did the Colts decide to keep Taylor? Here's what to know about their decision as they move forward with the star running back in tow.

Week 3: Denver Broncos cheerleaders perform during the "Sunday Night Football" game against the Los Angeles Rams at Empower Field at Mile High.

The Colts didn't trade Taylor because they view the 27-year-old running back as a part of their future.

Colts coach Shane Steichen spoke glowingly of Taylor when asked what it mean to keep him in the fold during a media availability the day following the 2026 NFL trade deadline.

"He means a ton. He's a hell of a football player. I'm excited that he's here," Steichen told reporters Nov. 11. "It's awesome for the organization to keep, you know one of the best running backs in the league in your franchise moving forward."

Taylor has certainly thrived across his seven seasons to date with the Colts. He has twice led the NFL in total touchdowns during his career, earned an All-Pro first-team nod in 2021, and is pacing for his fourth season with at least 1,500 scrimmage yards in 2026.

Taylor is also under contract through the 2028 NFL season, so the Colts won't have to worry about locking him down to another deal while he remains in the prime of his career.

That may have been part of the reason the Colts were only willing to consider moving Taylor in a "blockbuster offer," per The Ringer's Tom Pelissero. The Colts received packages including a second-round pick and more for Taylor, but he remains under team control and can continue to serve as Indianapolis' offensive engine as the team rebuilds.

It's also worth noting that Steichen expressed no worries about the relationship between Taylor and the franchise even after the persistent trade rumors surrounding the star running back.

"I can't speak for him, but I know that last week, he said he's proud to wear the horseshoe," Steichen said. "And so like I said, I'm excited that he's here. He's a great freaking football player."

And Taylor reiterated in a media availability of his own that he was happy to remain in Indianapolis.

"No," Taylor replied when asked whether he wanted to be traded before the deadline. "Just regular scheduled programing Tuesday. Like it's all water under the bridge now."

While Taylor remained with the Colts, the 27-year-old may not handle his usual workload down the stretch of the 2026 NFL season.

The Colts are sitting at 3-7 entering Week 11 and are highly unlikely to make any sort of playoff push during the second half of the season. As a result, the Colts have seemingly started to limit Taylor's workload, as he has averaged 17.1 touches per game since Week 7 while playing no more than 69% of the offensive snaps in any game.

By comparison, Taylor averaged 22.8 touches per game and never played fewer than 71% of the team's offensive snaps across the first six games of the season.

Taylor's slight downtick in production has coincided with an uptick in production from rookie backup Seth McGowan. The seventh-rounder averaged just four touches per game across the first six games of the season but has seen that number balloon to nine per game in his last four outings.

Against the Dolphins, Taylor saw 18 touches while McGowan had 12 and scored a touchdown. That 60-40 workload split could end up being closer to the norm moving forward as the Colts look to keep Taylor fresh and healthy during what looks like a lost season.

Below is a look at the Colts' running back depth chart, which remains headlined by Taylor even amid his recent downtick in touches per game.

Giddens, a fifth-round pick in the 2025 NFL Draft, has played sparingly this season, logging six carries for 43 yards. All of those touches game in Indianapolis' 42-7 blowout loss to the Jacksonville Jaguars in Week 8.

Meanwhile, Booth is an undrafted free agent who currently resides on the Colts' practice squad. He spent the offseason with the Cleveland Browns before joining Indianapolis' practice squad on Sept. 1.

Taylor is playing the 2026 NFL season on the final year of a three-year, $42 million extension he inked with the Colts in 2023. After this season, the two-year, $44 million extension he signed with Indianapolis on Aug. 6, 2026, will officially kick in.

Below are the full details of the latter agreement, which will keep Taylor under contract with the Colts through the 2028 season. All details are via Spotrac.com.

Taylor's $22 million AAV ranks third league-wide among running backs. Only Jahmyr Gibbs ($22.5 million) and Bijan Robinson ($22.5 million) are making more.

#2971. Lakers coach has savage reaction to embarrassing loss to lowly Jazz

JJ Redick entered the Lakers' locker room on Wednesday night following the team's 109-97 loss to the Jazz at Crypto.com Arena with a simple question.

Who on the team felt like they played well in the Lakers' first loss in nearly three weeks?

There was no sugarcoating Wednesday's loss.

"We didn't play well," Redick said. "Nobody played well. It's not Walker, it's not Luka, it's not Q, it's not Sandro [Mamukelashvili]. Nobody had a great game."

Los Angeles Lakers head coach JJ Redick reacts during a game against the Utah Jazz at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles on Wednesday, Nov. 18, 2026. Getty Images

Luka Doncic #77 of the Los Angeles Lakers attempts a shot against Mo Bamba #44 of the Utah Jazz. Getty Images

A quick look at the box score explains why.

Yes, Luka Dončić had 29 points, seven assists and five rebounds, but fatigue appeared to catch up to him – evident by his nine turnovers, 10-for-28 shooting from the field and his streak of scoring at least 30 points ending.

Yes, Quentin Grimes followed up his 47-point masterpiece during the win over the Hornets with a 20-point showing against the Jazz, but he scored two points and shot just 1 for 6 from the field during a fourth quarter the Lakers were outscored 31-21 on Wednesday.

Yes, Walker Kessler extended his double-double streak to six games, but he was the first to admit his scoring touch wasn't present (he shot 4 for 10 on free throws).

Ace Bailey of the Utah Jazz drives to the basket during a game against the Los Angeles Lakers. NBAE via Getty Images

The Lakers weren't overreacting to their nine-game winning streak game ending to a Jazz toward the bottom of the Western Conference standings.

The Lakers recognize that there are going to be games where it won't be their night in an 82-game season

Wednesday was one of those nights, with their shooting from all over the court falling well below the standard they've established for themselves.

Luka Doncic of the Los Angeles Lakers passes in front of Josh Green, Mo Bamba and Svi Mykhailiuk of the Utah Jazz. Getty Images

"You can't overemphasize a loss because we're still in a good spot," Jake LaRavia said. "We're going to stay connected as a team. We're not going to let this loss get to our heads too much. We got another opportunity Friday to bounce back."

Kessler added: "With 82 games, when you have a bad game like this, you don't want to spend too much time lingering on the losses. You want to learn from it and move on because you have to. Our offense, we were getting good looks. Just wasn't falling necessarily. So I don't think there's cause for concern."

#2970. NFL puts product over principles with Puka Nacua suspension | Opinion

The NFL is tarnishing its precious shield with its suspension* of Puka Nacua – even more than the Los Angeles Rams wide receiver did with his reprehensible behavior.

The league has known since March that Nacua was being sued by a woman who said the player had bitten her on the shoulder during a New Year's Eve dinner, where he also allegedly said something antisemitic. But it wasn't until Thursday, Nov. 19, when Nacua is conveniently already on injured reserve, that the NFL saw fit to suspend him.

Follow along while I do the math: NFL players on IR have to miss a minimum four games before they can return. Nacua, who missed the first of those four games last week while dealing with a knee injury, is now suspended for the next three weeks.

Which means Nacua won't miss any more time than the Rams were already accounting for, and they – and the league – will get one of their biggest and most-productive stars back, healthy and rested, just in time for 6-4 Los Angeles to make a push for the playoffs.

Unless you're a Rams fan, that stinks to high heaven.

Ricky Pearsall, SF (knee) - Out for seasonRicky Pearsall of the San Francisco 49ers works out during training camp practice at SAP Performance Facility on July 27, 2026, in Santa Clara, California.

Chris Brazzell, CAR (knee) - Out for seasonChris Brazzell II of the Carolina Panthers warms up during the Carolina Panthers Training Camp at Carolina Panthers Practice Fields on July 23, 2026, in Charlotte, North Carolina.

Jayden Higgins, HOU (knee) - Out for seasonJayden Higgins of the Houston Texans catches a pass for a touchdown during the second quarter of the game against the Indianapolis Colts at NRG Stadium on January 04, 2026, in Houston, Texas.

Ricky Pearsall, SF (knee) - Out for seasonRicky Pearsall of the San Francisco 49ers works out during training camp practice at SAP Performance Facility on July 27, 2026, in Santa Clara, California.

Chris Brazzell, CAR (knee) - Out for seasonChris Brazzell II of the Carolina Panthers warms up during the Carolina Panthers Training Camp at Carolina Panthers Practice Fields on July 23, 2026, in Charlotte, North Carolina.

Jayden Higgins, HOU (knee) - Out for seasonJayden Higgins of the Houston Texans catches a pass for a touchdown during the second quarter of the game against the Indianapolis Colts at NRG Stadium on January 04, 2026, in Houston, Texas.

What Nacua did was apparently bad enough to merit a three-game suspension, but not bad enough for the NFL to shut him down when it would actually matter? So what, exactly, is the point of suspending Nacua at all? Because it sure doesn't tell him, or anyone else, that there are consequences for unacceptable behavior.

(Nacua's suspension* will cost him close to $1 million in lost salary. That'll be pocket change when he signs a new contract in the offseason, to say nothing of the endorsement money he's still getting.)

Apparently, the story the NFL is spinning is that if Nacua messes up again, the NFL disciplinarians are really going to let him have it! They pinky swear it!

Yes, the leagues' collective bargaining agreement with its players allows players to serve suspensions while on IR. That's not the point. The point is everyone has known for almost nine months that Nacua was going to be suspended, and the NFL just so happened to choose the least-inconvenient time possible, for both Nacua and the Rams, to do it. A time that helps preserve Los Angeles' hopes for a Super Bowl 61 appearance at SoFi Stadium, its home arena.

Other suspensions have been dragged out because of ongoing legal cases. That's not an issue, here, however. Nacua was never charged criminally. The civil lawsuit against him has not been resolved. If the NFL has already waited nine months to hand down punishment everyone was expecting, what difference would it have made for the league to wait another three weeks?

Except for the impact it would have on the Rams' playoff chances, that is, and the all-important postseason intrigue for a major-market NFL team.

It is also true that other players have served suspensions while on IR. According to Adam Schefter, Daniel Ekuale was given five games for violating the performance-enhancing substances policy after suffering a season-ending injury last year. In 2024, Sam Williams was suspended three games for violating the personal conduct policy while out for the season with a torn ACL.

Mike Woods got six games in 2023 for violating the personal conduct policy when he was already out with a torn Achilles. In 2018, David Parry was suspended for four games for violating both the personal conduct and PED policies when his season had already ended because of an ankle injury.

What's the difference between them and Nacua? Ekuale, a defensive lineman, spent seven years in the league, making 21 starts in 64 games and recording six sacks. Williams, a defensive end, has five starts in 58 games over four seasons. Woods, a receiver, had 12 catches in 15 games. Parry, a two-season starter at nose tackle in Indianapolis, played in four games his final two seasons.

Nacua is a two-time Pro Bowler who last year led the league with 129 catches and also had 1,715 yards receiving and 10 touchdowns. He's started all but one game he's played in his first four seasons, including seven this year. Despite missing last week, Nacua is fourth in the NFL with 70 catches.

Nacua shouldn't be punished more harshly because he's one of the NFL's top players. But neither should his star status earn him any favors. The NFL is insulting everyone's intelligence by claiming to take anything that reflects poorly on the league very, very seriously and then turning around and making sure Nacua's punishment won't cause anyone any real hardship.

The NFL's justice system has always been arbitrary. Turns out, it's not very just, either.

Follow USA TODAY Sports columnist Nancy Armour on social media @nrarmour.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: NFL puts product over principles with Puka Nacua suspension | Opinion

#2968. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2962. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.

Anmuth ranks No. 832 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of about 9.40%. See Meta Platforms Financials on TipRanks.

#2961. Dr. J, Julius Randle rip into Nets after embarrassing loss to Bulls

This was a night the Brooklyn Nets could feel good and get a win. It was a night honoring the 1974 and 1976 ABA championship teams, and the struggling Chicago Bulls were in town, a game the Nets could, and maybe should, win.

Instead, the Nets got their doors blown off early and went on to lose by 30, 137-107.

The Nets broadcast brought on Julius Irving, the legendary Dr. J and greatest player in franchise history — the star of those championship teams — and he was the first to rip the team and the way the day was handled.

"We didn't really talk to the players," Irving said on the YES Network broadcast. "You know, their coach was doing all the talking, and they were getting their preparation in, doing their drills, and so on and so forth. And I fully anticipated the Nets to be so fired up in having us here that they would blow Chicago out, but I'm looking at 62-39. So I was wrong."

After the game, Julius Randle, one of the players brought in to lift the floor of this team, was much harsher, via Brian Lewis at the New York Post.

"That was embarrassing. Been in the league for 13 years, and that's one of the worst games I've ever been a part of. And not because of the score... but it's how we did it. That wasn't cool. Nothing about it was cool," Julius Randle said. "We've got to find a way to figure out the root of it or whatever, but that's just unacceptable...

"It's just a pride, an effort, the competitive spirit, all of that, it was nonexistent."

"Very disappointed, and it was just starting with effort. We were not a competitive group, we were not a connected group, we were not a selfless group," Nets coach Jordi Fernández said. "So, I saw selfishness, I saw pointing fingers, and I saw guys that didn't always play hard."

The Nets are 3-11 in a season where they don't even control their own first-round pick (Houston has swap rights). The team is rebuilding and wasn't expected to be fighting for a playoff spot, but with Randle and Michael Porter Jr. on the roster, the expectations were higher than this.

#2958. Puka Nacua’s suspension won’t impact his availability for Seahawks games

Marred with injuries, it's been a frustrating season for the Los Angeles Rams' wide receiver corps thus far and already without its top two pass catchers — their situation nearly just got worse.

Puka Nacua has been suspended three games without pay for violating the league's personal conduct policy, the NFL announced on Thursday. In addition, the league reportedly communicated to the superstar wideout that any further violation "will subject him to additional and more substantial discipline, including banishment from the League."

The move stems from an apparent incident on New Year's Eve 2025, where a woman alleged he bit her on the shoulder and made an antisemitic remark. Although she filed a civil lawsuit against Nacua on March 25, his attorney denied any remarks were made and classified the alleged assault as "horseplay."

Despite currently being on injured reserve with MCL and PCL sprains as well as turf toe, the suspension will take effect immediately — making Nacua eligible to be reinstated ahead of Los Angeles' Week 15 game against the Dallas Cowboys — one week before they're set to visit the Seattle Seahawks.

It currently remains unclear if he'll be cleared to suit up when first eligible to return.

However, considering the Rams placed him on injured reserve ahead of Week 10, he was already poised to miss a minimum of four weeks. In essence, Nacua won't miss any time as a result of the suspension.

As for the financial punishment, he was fined $961, 167 — a sixth of his 2026 base salary. There is precedent for players like Nacua getting suspended while on IR, although the other examples are not nearly as prominent in the NFL.

In his continued absence, Los Angles will seemingly continue to lean on a mix of veterans and far less experienced receivers — although WR Davante Adams (ruptured hamstring) could potentially return in the coming weeks and take on the lead role until Nacua is eligible to take the field.
