#2952. Luka Dončić says fatigue can’t be an excuse for Lakers loss to Jazz

Playing their third game in four nights, the Los Angeles Lakers saw their nine-game winning streak come to an end Wednesday when they fell at home to the Utah Jazz, 109-97. Plenty of players struggled, including the Lakers' franchise superstar, Luka Dončić, who tallied 29 points on 10-of-28 shooting (2-of-12 from deep), seven assists and nine turnovers.

Following the loss, Dončić took accountability for his lackluster game and refused to let tired legs be the reason behind Los Angeles' poor performance.

"That can't be an excuse. All NBA team plays 82 games, so that can't be an excuse," Dončić said. "I got to do way better. I know that. I feel like we had some open shots we didn't make, but it's one game. We got to move on."

Dončić's 10-of-28 shooting (35.7%) marked his worst field goal percentage of the year while his 29 points were his fewest since Oct. 23 and his nine turnovers were his second-most this season. If the Lakers are going to contend for a championship like they aspire to, their best player, who rarely has this year, cannot sink to that level — and he knows it.

"I mean, honestly, I just got to be way better than this, Dončić said. "This is unacceptable for my part. I've got to lead the team in better ways. I look at this game, got to move on and learn from this."

Dončić's head coach, JJ Redick, refused to single out one player for the loss Wednesday. Dončić may be their superstar and most impactful contributor but he was far from the lone guy to play significantly below the standard they've set for themselves this season.

"I asked the guys, 'Who in here feels like they played well tonight?' Nobody raised their hand. Everybody could be better," Redick told reporters postgame. "We can sit here and nitpick every single guy. Nitpick me. It's whatever. Could my rotations have been different? Maybe [could have] gotten a little more offense, maybe played a little more size at the five when Jaxson [Hayes] or [Mo] Bamba was [out there]? Yeah. We'll do all that, but we didn't play well. Nobody played well. It's not Walker, it's not Luka, it's not Q, it's not Sandro. Nobody had a great game."

#2945. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2942. Aidan Hutchinson shares thoughts on injury: ‘I always come back stronger’

Detroit Lions All-Pro defensive end Aidan Hutchinson took to social media to deliver a message to fans for the first time since tearing his ACL in Week 10 against the New England Patriots in Germany. Hutchinson's injury occurred moments after a drive-ending sack of QB Drake Maye, as the star edge rusher leaped in celebration and his knee buckled on landing. The perennial Defensive Player of the Year candidate will require surgery, and his 2026 season is unfortunately over.

Here's the message Hutchinson wanted people to see:

I honestly can't believe it. I've jumped hundreds of times on a football field and never in a million years did I think this would happen to me.

But even something like this won't hold me back.

I can ask why over and over again, but it'll never make sense. All I can do is surrender to the path. Only God knows why and I trust in Him.

Very grateful for all of your prayers and well wishes.

Two things I know for sure:1. I always come back stronger.2. That's the last time I go airborne 😁👍

Hutchinson is no stranger to recovering from season-ending injuries. In 2024, he broke his leg and spent several "miserable" days in a Dallas hospital, and gained a new perspective on life. Early signs for Hutchinson are that he didn't suffer any other significant knee damage, but they can't be certain until the surgery takes place. If it goes well, he's expected to have a chance to recover and return to play sometime in 2027, hopefully "stronger," as he did after his 2024 injury.

#2941. Anthony Edwards: 'I never wanted to have surgery,' no timeline for return

Anthony Edwards met with the media Thursday, but that doesn't mean he'll be back on the court later that night when the Timberwolves host the Knicks. When will he return?

"I have no clue. Whenever my knee tells me it's ready, I'll be ready," Edwards said in his first public words since he had arthroscopic surgery to clean up his right knee three weeks ago. The surgery is expected to keep Ant sidelined until around Christmas.

Edwards said he didn't really want to have the surgery, but had to after the pain got worse.

"I'm the type of guy, I never wanted to have surgery. I thought it would just, you know, go away. In the offseason it was actually good, like we was managing it pretty good, treating it, and it wasn't as swole. Then, as soon as the season kicked off it, I don't know what happened. It just flared back up like really bad. Then I was like, "Man, it's time"...

"It was good in [training] camp, like I said. I think after the Miami game it started to flare up, and then it went down a little bit after the Orlando game, so we thought we were going to be alright, but then after the back-to-back it was really bad, like painful to fly, so I'm like, "I gotta get it."

It's a small sample size, but Edwards arguably was playing the best basketball of his career to start the season, averaging 33.3 points, 4.8 rebounds and 3.5 assists per game while shooting 40% from 3-point range, all while the Timberwolves were 3-1.

Minnesota, behind LaMelo Ball, Ayo Dosunmu and the best defense in the league, has gone 5-2 since Edwards went out. Edwards recognized what is going on and said that when he returns he wants to fit in.

"I'm thirsty to come back, but not super fast because of how they're playing. I don't want to take them out of rhythm," Edwards said. "But I told them, when I come back, I'm not going to try to be who I usually am and try to ball hog and take all the shots. I'm just going to fit in where I get in – take a couple catch-and-shoots. When they need me, I'm there."

Edwards isn't just going to accept a role, he's an elite player, but Minnesota might be better — and a more dangerous playoff team — because of what they have learned while he is out.

#2934. Federal Reserve issues FOMC statement

An official website of the United States Government

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2930. What we learned as Warriors' 3-point shooting stays silent in loss to Grizzlies

What we learned as Warriors' 3-point shooting stays silent in loss to Grizzlies originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

A 28-point loss to the Memphis Grizzlies on Thursday night salted the Warriors' wounds and capped a winless five-game road trip.

The Warriors lost Steph Curry in the first few minutes of the trip and never found an answer after. The final straw was a 126-98 loss at FedExForum. The Warriors failed to reach 100 points in the final four games of the road trip and lost by at least 20 points each time.

Charles Bassey was the Warriors' surprise leading scorer, finishing with a 16-point, 13-rebound double-double.  Thursday marked the first game all season where the Warriors didn't have a single 20-point scorer.

They continued to be doomed by poor defense and terrible starts.

Here are three takeaways from the Warriors' fifth straight loss.

In summer league, Yaxel Lendeborg exacted his revenge on Cam Boozer by avenging his one loss in Las Vegas with a win against the Grizzlies in the championship game. Boozer got the best of Lendeborg in their first regular season game against each other, and it was the same result the second time.

Lendeborg was a game-low minus-31 with 11 points on 3-of-11 shooting, but he did have eight rebounds, seven assists and two blocked shots. Boozer was much better as a plus-16 with 16 points on a perfect 6-of-6 shooting and made all four of his free throws. The much-younger Boozer also added six rebounds and five assists.

The game looked easy to Boozer, and a struggle for Lendeborg – mentally and physically.

After using size to his advantage in Dallas, Lendeborg floated far too often around the 3-point line. It's his teammates' job to find him on mismatches. It's up to Lendeborg first to create them, though.

As the Warriors suffered yet another blowout loss, Will Richard again was a silver lining.

Richard the night before in Dallas scored 20 points in 27 minutes. He was a plus-8 in a 22-point loss, going 9 of 13 from the field with four steals. That same energy followed Richard to Memphis, where he scored 15 points off the bench on 5-of-8 shooting.

Without Curry's gravity and with Lendeborg struggling, the Warriors' best offense has been a heavy dose of cutting. Richard is among the Warriors' best cutters, and it's how he immediately made an impact. His teammates should take note.

In a back-to-back where not much went right for the Warriors, Richard scored 35 points off the bench on 14-of-21 shooting (66.7 percent).

Al Horford met with media members during Warriors shootaround in San Antonio and said the Warriors' best bet of survival without Curry is defense. The Warriors have to lean on defense and build off strong starts if they want to compete. They couldn't accomplish either in Memphis, or the rest of the road trip.

Awful starts continued with the Warriors down 31-24 after the first quarter, which is an improvement as of late. But their process was not. The Warriors in the first quarter made eight shots and turned the ball over eight times for 13 points by the Grizzlies.

Golden State surrendered 22 turnovers, which led to 35 points on the other side. The Warriors' entire starting five had multiple turnovers, including three from Lendeborg and Gui Santos. Turnovers are just one part of how the Warriors were outscored by 80 points in the first quarter (174-94) on an 0-5 road trip.

The offense taking a tumble in Curry's absence is understandable. The Warriors' defense being this bad isn't. The Grizzlies shot 52.2 percent from the field, 42.9 percent from three and made all 17 of their free throws while barely breaking a sweat.

#2927. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2922. Not extending Ja’Quan McMillian would be a mistake

The news breaking of the $67M contract extension for cornerback Riley Moss was well-received by most fans. As it should, he's been the primary focus of targets with Pat Surtain II opposite him and he has held up as well as any number one guy on any defense. He probably could have gotten close to $20M a year and Denver got him for $16.75. A very good deal.

That said, it would still be a mistake for the Denver Broncos to let fellow cornerback Ja'Quan McMillian walk in free agency. McMillian is just a playmaker and you cannot put a price tag on that or find any easy replacement. They will have same issue if/when Marvin Mims Jr. walks, although he has been a bit quiet this year so far on the playmaker side of things.

We asked fans a month ago who they felt the Broncos should keep if they only kept one and the vote was quite literally one-sided:

The funny part about that is even those of us who voted McMillian would also love to see Riley Moss around too. He has been a very good outside cornerback, but McMillian has made the big plays in key moments. Those monster plays stick with you when you think about who has made a bigger impact.

Hopefully Denver finds a way to keep both and gets McMillian locked down long-term ahead of free agency next year.

Broncos vs. Raiders: 3 Things to Watch in Week 11

The Denver Broncos host the Las Vegas Raiders on Sunday. Here are three storylines I'll be watching closely in their Week 11 matchup.

How the Denver Broncos can beat the Las Vegas Raiders

In order for the Denver Broncos to defeat the Las Vegas Raiders on Sunday, here are three things they need to accomplish.

Broncos looking to start second half of season on a high note

The Denver Broncos are back to business this week and will host the Las Vegas Raiders on Sunday. Despite a 4-6 record, Head Coach Sean Payton knows they'll be a tough team to beat.

On if he can tell what the bye week did for the team physically

"It's hard to tell. I think you never can control obviously your bye. I'm sure there are a handful of players that may not have been able to play had we have played last Sunday that will have a chance of playing this Sunday. I thought we had good work. It was a good full practice longer than two weeks ago. Yes, it went well."

On if he knows Raiders Head Coach Klint Kubiak

"I know his father [former Broncos Head Coach Gary Kubiak]. I know he was here. [He's] one of the guys I spent a lot of time with. I have a ton of respect for their family and what he's doing. There are a lot of ties just to that organization. I think already you can see it on tape what they're wanting to build and the early success this year. You go through Kansas City, Seattle, Buffalo, these are all teams that are in the thick of it, and they played all those teams to one-score games."

On if the Raiders' offense has changed with a new starting quarterback

"There's a system they believe in though, and you still saw it in the game they played last week. So they're going to do a good job with the run game, the play-action off of it. Fortunately we've had a week at least to see tape on the new quarterback. You have to defend the offense and then obviously the people in it."

On what he likes about what the team is doing right now

"It's a little bit more than halfway in the season, and there's a lot that still needs to be done. I feel the same way about our team. We knew going in there was a challenge to the start of the season based on the opponents. You get so micro-focused on just the next game and the things that these guys do well. They return kicks well. They punt it well. They force fumbles better than anyone in the league. They're really good offensively, red zone. They'll possess the ball if you allow them to. They do a really good job of the time of possession. The takeaways have been noticeable. I don't know if that answers your question, but rarely do you really take a bigger picture look. You're aware of where your team's at, but then you're also very mindful of the things that we need to continue to improve on."

On how the offensive line can block better

"There are times in a game where there are going to be some looks that are tougher looks. There were some opportunities against Carolina where we could have been a little bit better, a little cleaner."

On how taking the whole bye week off benefits the team and his conversations with Chiefs Head Coach Andy Reid about it

"Remember it wasn't like we split the atom when he gave me that answer. It's a long season. I think guys get away mentally, and physically, the guys that were or currently are injured are here. It's good to have everyone back."

On Raiders QB Fernando Mendoza's first start last week

"It's a tough start obviously when you play the Super Bowl champion. I remember his college tape well. All of us that would go through the draft process. Obviously he's a winner. The things I see really are just from afar, but you see the quick release and all the things you're looking for in that position. I've heard nothing but great things from people that have been around him."

On if the evaluation of quarterbacks in the draft process is the same when there's not need at that position

"It is. We're not going to have a private workout, so maybe to some degree it isn't also. Fair question. I just remember what it was the year [QB] Bo [Nix] came out. The tape evaluation, the grades, the reading of the player, that's very much the same, the time you spent. You don't really try to hurry through position groups. You really try to put grades and discuss players, and you have plenty of time to do that."

On if the notion that rookie quarterbacks struggle in their first few starts is overblown

"There have been great debuts. You could go back and find an argument probably for a lot of [players], and a lot of it may be circumstantial relative to the surrounding cast. This clearly is a different team than we've played the last couple of years. That's the part where you see [Head Coach] Klint [Kubiak]'s imprint, the people that are there coaching from top down."

"It's good to get him out here. He's worked his tail off. Today was his first practice. He was making a lot of strides, and that setback was unfortunate, but he has that spirit, that will. You never felt like he lost interest. So it was good to see him moving around."

On if WR Courtland Sutton's ability to win a release in the short red zone has improved since he arrived

"It's a strength of his. He's real good at the line. His size, he uses his hands well. I think it's something that for him, being in the paint, if you will, as a power player is something that he excels at."

On if he looks for the ability to release when grading receivers before the draft

"I think you're looking for stop start, you're looking for transition. I like bigger receivers in our league period. That doesn't mean entirely, but the target radius and all those things are quarterback friendly with him."

On if Lions DE Aidan Hutchinson's injury during a celebration is a teaching moment

"I'm kind of with [Lions Head Coach] Dan [Campbell]. I saw the final comments. We're teachers and we're constantly… But we're going to walk out of the doors, and there've been a lot of celebrations. I'm kind of with Dan there where it's unfortunate. Two good players got hurt on the same play, and I think that there are a lot of other things that I would put on tape on a Friday, Saturday that are [teaching moments]. That, I wouldn't put that on."

On if he takes pride in watching a former player and coach like Lions Head Coach Dan Campbell handle adversity

"Yes, absolutely. He was that way… I was a young assistant with the Giants when we drafted him out of [Texas] A&M. I was with him in New York his first four years. Then I go to Dallas. I kind of speak on his behalf, we sign him there. We're together there. We counted the years once. There was a clip where he was somewhere else, then at Miami and then back coaching. He's always been gritty, tough, dependable, reliable—all the traits that you admire as a player, you see now as a coach, as a communicator. Very quickly I think that team has kind of taken on his personality, and it's been a real good fit for that city. He's real authentic."

"Yes, I watched Sunday. I watched the Seattle game, and then the ones prior, not maybe as much."

"The TV copy gives you a different perspective. You hear the audio relative to injuries, and you're watching. It's different. When you put the all-22 on, you're like, 'OK. I didn't get that with this play, but I see it now.' So it's a different watch. Nonetheless, I think it's beneficial."

On if he ever watches the TV copy when reviewing film

"Believe it or not, in our cut-ups, you can set this setting—mine is set right now—to go wide copy, end-zone copy, end-zone copy, TV copy. So if I want to see the TV copy or hear the audio of something, I can. I usually click through it, but sometimes if there's something that I… If I see an opposing quarterback maybe look like he audibled now that we put microphones everywhere, we have to change them… So I'll go to the TV copy to see if I heard was he changing a play or not, that kind of thing."

On if he pays attention to the TV analysts while watching

"Not really. I know that there are a lot of games televised. The only big difference for me is years and years in the NFC, there was more FOX. Then here, much more CBS. I would say that's the noticeable difference."

On if he saw anything while reviewing film that he wants QB Bo Nix to improve on

"I think the one thing that's changed… Fair question. The old bye week was the collection of… We get such updated information, it's now do you want now's information the week you're not playing because you had it last… In other words, it's much more available, quicker each week. There'll be a self-scout we pay attention to. I like how he's playing, progressing. We keep looking at that."

On being on the ManningCast this week and the reaction from his teammates

"It was fun. They (Pro Football Hall of Fame QB Peyton Manning and former Giants QB Eli Manning) do a really good job. Obviously, I've known those guys for a while. They always have great attitudes and are great guys to talk to. They are extremely funny and they have great personalities. At the same time, they are the Mannings, so they have great football minds, too. It's fun to get on and talk with those guys. It was a great environment and easy conversation. It was all good."

On how he feels about the offense trending in the right direction

"I think even if we were playing great that we would feel really good. I think with the numbers and stats, we will always want more. That's the thing about stats is that they are never good enough. For us, it's always about the win column and being efficient and helping our team by playing complimentary football. Overall during the season, we've done that. When we've needed to score, we've scored. When we've needed a stop, we get a stop and then our special teams are playing really well. Overall as a team, we are playing well and complimentary off of each other. We just need to finish strong. We haven't really done much yet, so we have to keep going."

On if there is an added sense of urgency coming off the bye week entering the second part of the year

"You're refreshed and you get going, so you have a second wind. I think there is something to that. The first part of the season is really tough coming out of the gate after training camp, so you get to the bye and that is always a point to regroup and go from there. I think we've put ourselves in a spot to now make the push and really get going. There is an element to the peak of the season. We're almost to that point and almost to the end in December and January, where the games get really important. Not that they weren't already, but those have playoff implications and then you run into the playoffs. You want to be confident going into those. Right now is the stretch as a team that we can't let off and really continue to press."

On if he believes the offense has gotten into a groove the last few weeks

"We have to keep progressing and everyone on the team will tell you that there is room for growth. I'm not too worried about the things that come with the wins. It's all about, 'Did I put my team in a spot to win the game?'"

On his connection with WR Courtland Sutton

"No. 1, he does a great job of using his physicality down in the red zone. He's a big body, so that helps. When the field gets tighter, he is able to go up for the jump balls and he is able to create space. Then at the end of the day when the game is on the line and it's red zone time, he just knows that is his area and where he is strongest at… When I get to him and he's open, that's always the best part of the play. I know he's going to come down with the catch in the end."

On if his mentality of peaking at the right moment helps him get through the season

"Absolutely. In the NFL, there are so many weeks that you have to put your head down and just keep working. One week could look completely different and one week doesn't tell the story for the next week. For me, it's a marathon and not a sprint. You can't wear yourself out early. You don't want to peak too soon. It's just sort of the game within the game of the season. It's finding ways to get better so that when that moment comes and the playoffs are here and when you have to win the football game, you're not going home early because you missed out on your peak. Coming off the bye, we feel fresh, we feel good and ready for this next stretch."

On the environment at Empower Field at Mile High for opposing offenses and quarterbacks

"We have a pretty tough environment for opposing quarterbacks. It's never easy going on the road against our crowd and obviously against our defense. That's going to be big-time this weekend, just affecting the quarterback and communicating will be hard as well. It's always tough going against our crowd, which is an advantage for us."

On the challenge of playing against Raiders TE Brock Bowers

"Some people think that he's a tight end, I think he's a receiver the way they utilize him in the route tree. I feel like his route tree and his yards per target is very telling. They utilize him in a multitude of ways that you have to be keyed in about. It's all about honing in on the details and understanding how they're trying to use him."

On what he sees from the Raiders offense this season under Head Coach Klint Kubiak

"It's a different offense, like more of that West Coast offensive feel. They want to get started on the run game, and that leads to the play-action and stuff like that. It opens up their offense even more. They utilize a whole lot of personnel, 13, 12, 11, whatever how you look at it. It's going to be a test for us just to get lined up in matching personnel."

On what he sees from the Raiders this season

"Someone asked me this weekend about divisional games. I tell you all the time, every game weighs as heavy if it's the next one, but divisional games obviously carry a little bit more weight in the overall standings of things. The one thing that I've learned, noticed and have witnessed myself is it doesn't matter what a team's record is when you're talking about divisional games because they're going to show up and play hard no matter what. The Raiders have always been that team. No matter what their record is, they always show up and play the Broncos really hard. I wouldn't expect them to do anything different. To answer your question, on film you can see. You watch the games, and they're in a lot of these games against really good teams. Record may not show it, but that's a really good team that plays really hard. Going back to [Raiders Head] Coach [Klint [Kubiak], they have a really good leader over there. I wouldn't expect them to come in and do anything else than to give us their best."

On the wide receiver group's mentality and how guys have stepped up

"I think we got to see it at different parts of the season so far in different games, if a guy goes down here, if a guy goes down there. The thing that I appreciate and I love about our room is the next-man-up mentality. You never want to see your brother go down, but whenever someone does go down, we still have to go out there and perform. To see guys go out there and not only know what to do, but to go out there and perform at a high level… Some of the plays that [WR] Troy [Franklin] executed in the game, he didn't even practice throughout the week. He just went out there, and he knew the job assignment, and he went out there and made plays. 'Marv' (WR Marvin Mims Jr.) as well. Some of the things that 'Marv' was doing were some of the plays that were for [WR] Jaylen [Waddle] and Jaylen got hurt. 'Marv' stepped up and knew what to do. Troy stepped up and knew what to do. [WR] Pat Bryant had a really good game. I think our room—and I say it all the time—our room is very deep. We have a lot of talent in our room. A lot of people may not know a lot of names in there, but their names will continue to get buzzed around the league because whenever it's time for them to step up and go out there and perform, they do that. I appreciate that for our room because it allows us to be able to be more versatile."

On tying Ring of Fame WR Demaryius Thomas for the fifth-most starts at wide receiver in team history

"It's humbling. Anytime someone tells me my name getting buzzed around anything that he [Ring of Fame WR Demaryius Thomas] has done, it's truly humbling and heartwarming because I know how much he means to this franchise, and organization and to this city. To be able to have that many games under my belt, which honestly shocked me. I had no clue before literally I came up here on the podium that it's going to be 125 games. I was like, 'Daggum.' I didn't know that. You go into each season, you want to be healthy. You want to do your best but then also you're chasing a Super Bowl. You don't think of how many games, this amount of games, that amount of games. It stacks up. It's truly an honor. I'm truly humbled to be able to have that milestone be crossed and for it to be around the same time as my brother being here as well. I'm grateful. Definitely grateful."

#2921. Timberwolves star Anthony Edwards speaks on decision to undergo knee surgery, has no clear return timeline: 'Whenever my knee tells me it's ready, then I'll be ready'

Three weeks after undergoing arthroscopic surgery on his ailing knee, Minnesota Timberwolves star Anthony Edwards is speaking out about his recovery.

On Thursday, the guard talked to reporters for the first time since the procedure, saying that the rehabilitation process was "going great" and that he was focused on improving the strength in his leg post-surgery. Edwards also spoke about the timeline of his injury before deciding to go under the knife, saying that his knee had been bothering him on and off for years but that it had "flared up" significantly last month.

"It was frustrating, but I realized I had to do it, so I just made my mind up that same day, like we're gonna do it tomorrow," Edwards said.

The 25-year-old guard said that he had managed his knee well in the offseason and that it was still "good" in camp. But by the Timberwolves' season opener on Oct. 21, it was getting worse, and was "really bothering" him in the team's win over the Orlando Magic on Oct. 23.

Minnesota's back-to-back on Oct. 25 and Oct. 26, against the Toronto Raptors at home and the Cleveland Cavaliers away, is when it really started to be a problem: "After the back-to-back it was really bad, like painful to fly," Edwards said.

Edwards said that he hopes that the surgery will help mitigate the pain going forward.

"It was something that I needed, it needed to be done in order for me to be able to play without any pain for the next couple years," Edwards said. "But who knows, maybe in a couple years I might have to get something else, but hopefully not. I think I'll be pretty good."

Edwards, who said that this is the longest he's ever gone without playing basketball, added that he plans on coming back this season but doesn't have a clear timeline. At the time of his surgery, reports indicated that he would need more than two months to recover.

"I have no clue. Whenever my knee tells me it's ready, then I'll be ready," he said of a return to the court.

Losing Edwards initially seemed to be a brutal blow to the Timberwolves' chances: Before the surgery, Edwards was averaging 33.3 points per game and seemed on track for another dominant season. But Minnesota has fared well in his absence, going 8-3 in the weeks since Edwards' surgery.

"I was thirsty to get back, especially after the first game," Edwards said, referring to the Timberwolves' loss to the Golden State Warriors right after his procedure. "I was ready to get back and just get in their ear, tell them what I'm seeing, feel the energy. But after that game, we kinda took off, so I was like, I'll take my time. But I'm happy to be around now."

While speaking to reporters, Edwards had high praise for all of his teammates, specifically pointing out the "phenomenal" play of Ayo Dosunmu — who currently leads the team in points — and noting that forward Jaden McDaniels is "stepping up big time." He also confirmed that he's been sharing his scouting reports with his teammates, staying involved in the team despite his absence from the court.

Led by Dosunmu and LaMelo Ball, Minnesota is currently fourth in the Western Conference with a 11-4 record and a pretty favorable schedule over the next few weeks.

#2913. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2912. Spurs guard Dylan Harper reportedly expected to miss up to 3 weeks with left rotator hip strain

San Antonio Spurs guard Dylan Harper has reportedly been diagnosed with a left hip rotator strain, ESPN's Shams Charania reports.

The recovery timetable for that injury is one to three weeks, depending on how Harper's rehabilitation progresses, according to Charania.

Harper, 20, sustained the injury during the Spurs' 105-93 loss to the Detroit Pistons on Tuesday. In the closing minute of the fourth quarter, Harper was about to receive a pass from Stephon Castle while matched up against Detroit's Ausar Thompson.

However, Harper's right foot slipped on the court and caused him to stretch awkwardly. He stayed down on the floor while play continued on the other end of the court, appearing to grab at his left hip or groin area.

The second-year guard was eventually able to stand up and walk off the court with some assistance from teammate Keldon Johnson. But he was noticeably limping and in pain as he went to the locker room. Harper did not return to the game during the final 53 seconds.

"It just looked like he slipped, obviously," Spurs coach Mitch Johnson told reporters afterward, via the San Antonio Express-News' Tom Orsborn. "So hoping for the best, but don't know anything yet."

San Antonio Spurs guard Dylan Harper slipped and suffered a hip injury late in Tuesday's loss to the Detroit Pistons.

Harper appeared at Thursday's shootaround with no noticeable limp, according to Orsborn. But that was apparently before imaging results came back from the team's medical staff.

"I'm happy he's not as hurt as he could be," said Spurs forward Julian Champagnie, who will replace Harper in the starting lineup. "Obviously, we need him, so finding out it wasn't that serious, or as serious as it could have been, was good news."

In 15 games this season, Harper is scoring 18.4 points per game, second on San Antonio behind Victor Wembanyama. He's also averaging 5.4 rebounds and 4.5 assists. He's shooting 50% from the floor and 31% on 54 3-point attempts.

At 12-3, the Spurs are a half-game behind the Los Angeles Lakers and Philadelphia 76ers for the NBA's top record. San Antonio hosts the Los Angeles Clippers (4-9) on Thursday night, then has two days off before hosting the New Orleans Pelicans (5-11) on Saturday.

#2911. Five reasons why 'Madden' movie fails miserably | Opinion

It's a line uttered by actor Nicolas Cage as he portrays the eponymous protagonist in "Madden." Unfortunately, he's central to a movie that fails to live up to that credo and doesn't seem to have much Commitment to Excellence in its bid to depict Pro Football Hall of Famer John Madden.

The movie, which began streaming on Prime Video on Wednesday, Nov. 18, opens with a black title card, which says, "Not all of this happened ... but most of it is true."

That effectively sets a tone for a movie that lacks drama, and − yep, authenticity. And while that might merely describes this movie's issues in broad brush strokes, "Madden" really flops for these five reasons:

While you might not think the 62-year-old Oscar winner would be a natural choice to play Madden, Cage put on 25 pounds and supplemented his weight gain with another 30 or so pounds of prosthetics to achieve something of a physical resemblance to the portly coach of the Oakland Raiders, TV analyst and entrepreneur.

Yet Cage's creative direction is baffling. He commits to his version of Madden's voice, but he sounds nothing like the man − to the point of distraction. But that's hardly the most jarring aspect of his performance.

Regardless of whatever phase of his career he was in, Madden was always an outsized character. And while he might have bordered on cartoonish as a coach, pitchman or, certainly at times, in the broadcast booth, he was nothing if not genuine and inimitable − plus brilliant in his various capacities. But Cage gives a rendition that's more a buffoonish caricature of an inattentive family man who dreams up football schemes with his head in a refrigerator (don't we all).

Anyone who draws a first impression of Madden, imperfections and all, from this movie isn't likely to form an image of the man described by his players, colleagues, friends and family through the decades.

David O. Russell directed "Madden," though NFL Films was involved with the project − and football aficionados will certainly recognize some vintage football sequences intercut into the film.

Otherwise? If you're not a football fan, "Madden" isn't likely to make you one.

The movie essentially starts with the 1976 AFC Championship Game, during which Madden's Raiders were poised to finally vanquish their archnemeses, the Pittsburgh Steelers, after years of falling short to the 1970s' preeminent dynasty. But in a failed bid to interject some sense of drama, the Steelers score first in "Madden," even though they never actually led in a game Oakland won 24-7 in reality. And while there's some acknowledgement of the tension that existed at the time between these franchises, "Madden" doesn't attempt to illustrate the backstory of Oakland's failures against the Steelers, including the "Immaculate Reception," Pittsburgh coach Chuck Noll suggesting there was a "criminal element" to the Raiders, nor is there much exploration of the colorful characters on both teams.

You might also come away thinking all football games are played inside stadiums with high concrete walls covered in bunting. Snippets of Cage using a telestrator? As silly as you might imagine. And scenes of actors playing football aren't value-added in the least − no reason to try and improve on NFL Films' capture of Hall of Famer Willie Brown's 75-yard pick-six in the Raiders' Super Bowl 11 blowout of the Minnesota Vikings, Madden's crowning achievement as a coach ... not that the point is driven home all that well in the movie.

Snoop Dogg and former NFL stars Marshawn Lynch and Julian Edelman appear in "Madden." None do much more than serve as set pieces, Snoop the only one to even get a few lines − those serving to convey the cultural phenomenon that is EA Sports' "Madden NFL," which Madden himself was instrumental in developing, marketing and legitimizing.

Seems like a squandered opportunity given the outsized personalities of this trio ... and, if I'm being honest, I didn't even realize Edelman was in the movie at all until I scanned the closing credits.

WORST SPORTS MOVIES: Will 'Madden' join their ranks?

But Christian Bale's depiction of Raiders owner Al Davis really stands out in this flick. Bale managed to replicate Davis' distinctive look, through various times in his life, as well as his signature Brooklyn accent. His relationship with his wife, Carol (played by Sienna Miller), comes across as far richer and more nuanced than Madden and his wife, Virginia − though Kathryn Hahn does a nice job in the role.

But watching Bale as the recalcitrant, paranoid, renegade boss of the Raiders − and Madden's lifelong friend, despite the strain football often put on their relationship − leaves the viewer wanting for more, especially since Davis' football legacy is equally impactful to Madden's if not as well known or understood in pop culture.

You'd think a movie about a larger-than-life persona like Madden's would be entertaining, at least largely so. Not the case here. "Madden" literally put me to sleep for 20 minutes even though its run time comes in shy of two hours. You'd think it would lend itself to humor. But that winds up being quite forced − whether it's Cage puking while battling an ulcer, Shane Gillis' fictional character Vince McGary as Madden's neighbor, or Cage and Bale listening to N.W.A. on the Madden Cruiser ... which hardly seems to serve a purpose and certainly doesn't much illustrate the Raiders' ethos, especially when they were based in Los Angeles at the time the hip-hop super group rose to prominence.

Want a gripping, accurate feel for John Madden? I'd point you to Fox's documentary "All Madden.," which premiered on Christmas Day 2021, just days before Madden's death.

Thankfully, that's the one the man himself got to see.

All the NFL news on and off the field. Sign up for USA TODAY's 4th and Monday newsletter.

#2909. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

#2904. Dylan Harper could miss 2-3 weeks with left hip external rotator strain

After the Spurs announced yesterday that Dylan Harper would miss today's game against the Clippers with "hip soreness" but were still waiting for more information, they have now revealed that he suffered a left hip external rotator strain and could miss up to 2-3 weeks (although it could be less if all goes well). While any injury is not ideal news, it is certainly much less serious than a groin injury, which was the initial fear, and that timeline for return is friendlier than the 4-6 weeks social media doctors were suggesting when the injury occurred.

Harper suffered the injury in the final minute of Tuesday's loss to the Pistons when he was running up the court and slipped, falling awkwardly into a splits and appearing to pull his groin. He stayed down for a bit before limping back to the locker room. This morning's shootaround brought some relief ahead of the Spurs releasing the medical results, as Julian Champagnie hinted that it was not as serious as initially feared, and he was seen walking around without a limp.

Harper has been the Spurs' second leading scorer this season after Victor Wembanyama, averaging 18.4 points on 50% shooting. #2898. Packers Roster: Green Bay’s emergency signing board

Throughout the season, the Green Bay Packers bring in players for tryouts to either push the current practice squad as an immediate signing or to build a board of emergency players if a few injuries start to build up in the future. Since it's the bye week, I decided to take a fresh look at all of the players that the Packers have brought in so far in 2026 (56 in total) and paint a picture of who is still available.

We'll break these tryout players down into five groups:

Players who are signed with other NFL teams

Players whose rights have been claimed by UFL teams

DL Haggai Ndubuisi – international exemption, PS

The Packers have signed two offensive linemen to their 53-man roster after tryouts (Kevin Zeitler and Mekhi Becton), but Becton was initially signed to the practice squad before being promoted up. Currently, 10 of Green Bay's 17 practice squad spots have been filled by players brought in on tryouts. The only practice squad players who have been with the team since training camp are WR Isaiah Neyor, TE Drake Dabney, OL Dalton Cooper, OL Dillon Wade, ED Arron Mosby, ED Nyjalik Kelly and CB Domani Jackson.

RB Damon Bankston (Vikings PS) – previously on Packers PS

OL Cordell Volson (Seahawks PS) – previously on Packers PS

None of these players are available for the Packers to sign to the practice squad, since they are already signed to another team's practice squad, but Green Bay could sign them to the 53-man roster as a poach. If the Packers sign another team's practice squad player, they must guarantee that player's pay and roster spot for three full weeks of the regular season.

DL David Blay Jr. – previously on Packers PS

DL Eric Johnson II – previously on Packers PS

ED Cruz Smith – international exemption eligible

S Kahlef Hailassie – previously on Packers PS

These are players who are completely on the street. If you want to think about the emergency board, these are the bones of that board right now.

The UFL just had its draft, which included teams selecting multiple Packers visitors to the league. Players with the "retained" label are 2026 UFL players whom Green Bay worked out, while players with the "draft" label are new players to the UFL whom the Packers worked out before they were picked in the recent draft.

Players who were picked in the recent draft are not all under contract with the league yet, so there's no guarantee they even play spring ball.

Usually, the UFL allows its players to seek NFL employment up to the week after the Super Bowl. If players sign reserve/futures contracts with NFL teams, though, they become ineligible to play in the upcoming UFL season, as they're under contract with another league.

This one is a special case. The Packers worked out cornerback Zy Alexander, coming off a season in the CFL, last week. Alexander cannot sign with an NFL team (would likely be a reserve/futures deal) until after the regular season is over, but he also cannot sign with an NFL team after the CFL free agency period starts in early February.

So right now, Alexander can't sign with an NFL club, but he will have a window when he can be added. He can currently work out with teams, though, as long as NFL clubs file a request with the player's CFL club (which can't be denied).

→ Baca artikel asli

#2897. This move will prove to pay off for the Suns

As we waited for the Phoenix Suns season to start, many questions arose ahead of the final decisions about the team. With them having 15 players under contract, one of them, Mark Williams, out for an extended period before the season starts, and only two centers on the roster, changes were inevitable. That is where we saw Haywood Highsmith get waived, giving the Suns flexibility with their 14-man roster, and we also saw moves on two two-way contracts. These were C.J. Huntley and Koby Brea, who were waived to add more size with Duop Reath and Drew Timme. Both Huntley and Brea remained with the Valley Suns after being waived, but they brought in two new faces who have both seen some time in the NBA.

Reath spent the last three years with the Portland Trail Blazers and brings versatility to the frontcourt with his shooting. In the past, I highlighted him as a low-end free-agent target for the Suns, and I am happy to see him in the Valley this year. Even if he is just there as insurance if another big goes down, the Suns need that, and Reath can provide valuable shooting to a bench unit that already excels at that if he does get an opportunity.

The name that went unnoticed, though, was Drew Timme, who joined the Suns after being waived by the Los Angeles Lakers. As with Jordan Goodwin last season, the Suns brought in someone from the Lakers, and this could prove a strategic move. After Goodwin paid off last offseason, the investment in Timme may also pay off, given his run in the G League so far.

Now, Timme is no stranger to the spotlight; he was a dominant collegiate player at Gonzaga before going undrafted in the 2023 draft. After bouncing around the Nets and Lakers, he now finds himself in the Valley with an opportunity to impress. Even though it is just the G League, these numbers prove that Timme is better than his current standing. We saw this trend with Collin Gillespie his first year in the Valley, and he turned out to be a key piece for the Suns. Timme could be that next buy-low product that the Suns invest in that pans out, and over the last few seasons, that is how they have found key depth pieces.

Both Gillespie and Bouyea were on two-way contracts before landing NBA deals, and with the Suns still having that open roster spot, could that happen by the end of the season? Only time will tell if Timme gets an opportunity, but with his recent play, I wouldn't rule it out. Especially with Mark Williams still out, the Suns lack some size in the frontcourt and have elected to give Koa Peat some small-ball minutes to replicate their third center for now. Could Timme end up fighting for that, depending on whether the Valley Suns are home? Personally, I'd like to see it.

Timme, even though he hasn't had overwhelming success in his 36 NBA games, has shown flashes of impact. Sadly, he played for a pretty poor Nets team, but with the Lakers, he made plays to help Luka Doncic and LeBron James. If you can get those quotes from the GOAT, that must mean something, right?

Timme can come in and be another solid passer and playmaker in this frontcourt. Like Ighodaro, he has the basketball IQ to make not only smart plays but the right ones, and he would help open up more opportunities for those bench scorers. The team is already deep, and as a two-way player, it is not shocking that he hasn't seen much time with the Suns yet, but if injuries open a spot, the Suns should give him that shot.

This shows the Suns' ability to build on the margins. They identify talent that can stand out, and even if it ruffles some feathers, they don't care. They will give up on draft picks or guys who haven't panned out if there is a better name out there, and you have to appreciate that from a business standpoint: they have gotten guys like Gillespie and Bouyea, and they only saw time with injuries. Timme's time may relate to that, but once again he is proving, like those guys, he is better than the G League, which is what the Suns want. They need these small-impact guys to pan out and be better than anticipated to build out the bench and depth, and they've done that.

All in all, as a fan of the team, it is nice to see them make these smart moves compared to the ones that have not worked. It shows they can succeed with these marginal moves and offers some hope that, if there is someone you don't know who is signed to this team, they may actually pan out.
