#2891. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

The Golden State Warriors' six-game road trip comes to an end Thursday night as they take on the Memphis Grizzlies on the second night of a back-to-back. The game will be played at 5:00 PM PT in Memphis and can be watched on NBC Sports Bay Area.

It was more of the same for Golden State during Wednesday night's 116-94 loss to the Dallas Mavericks. The Warriors struggled to generate much offense outside of the energy provided by Will Richard, who earned a spot in the starting lineup and finished with a team-high 20 points on 9-of-13 shooting from the field. The Warriors will look to salvage at least one win from this road trip with tonight's matchup against the Grizzlies. That being said, the impact of Steph Curry's absence continues to be felt throughout the lineup, particularly for rookie Yaxel Lendeborg, who has struggled to find his offensive rhythm in recent games.

Lendeborg thrived earlier this season as a secondary scorer and playmaker alongside Curry. Thursday's matchup presents an opportunity for Lendeborg to regain his rhythm as he goes up against familiar opponent in fellow rookie Cam Boozer. The Warriors narrowly lost their first meeting against Memphis earlier this season, with Lendeborg finishing that game with 18 points to Boozer's 25 points. If Golden State hopes to end its losing streak, getting Lendeborg going offensively while setting the tone defensively from the opening tip will be key to closing out this road trip on a positive note.

#2876. Latest Injury News Raises Concerns About Jets QB Geno Smith

For the second straight practice, New York Jets starting quarterback Geno Smith was listed as a limited participant with an ankle issue.

This has raised concerns about whether Smith will be able to play in Week 11 against the Los Angeles Chargers.

Smith told the media that "the plan" is for him to play this week, but "you obviously have to take it day-by-day."

The former West Virginia product refused to reveal whether he was dealing with an ankle sprain: "I won't get into the details."

"Now I was standing in the locker room, and Geno happened to walk in. I think he was trying to walk in quietly where people didn't see him. I saw him walking with a pretty pronounced limp, and he also had on a special shoe on his left foot, maybe an orthotic shoe, maybe some sort of slipper. He slipped out of that [and] then put on his regular sneakers to go over for the press conference with the media," ESPN's Rich Cimini said on the "Jets Collective" podcast.

Smith won't be 100% for this contest if he suits up. Cimini believes he is going to "push through it" and play.

"I would expect him to start. He is not going to be close to 100%. So they better protect him well because he doesn't move well to begin with and now he's on a bad wheel," Cimini said.

Two players didn't practice on Thursday, November 19, with wide receivers Omar Cooper Jr (ankle/hip) and Isaiah Williams (ribs). Neither player practiced on Wednesday either.

Cimini said this news "raises questions" about the Jets' wide receiver depth heading into Week 11.

ESPN's Todd Archer said, "Christian Parker will remain as Cowboys defensive coordinator but Mike Pettine, the senior defensive assistant, will call the defense on gamedays."

Pettine, 60, retired this offseason but was brought back off the couch to help the Dallas Cowboys.

"A dirty business. Mike Pettine, who gave Christian Parker his first NFL job, ends up replacing him on his first defensive coordinators job. Parker tried to bring Pettine in as a defensivev consultant in the spring and he declined. But in the fall Brian Schottenheimer and Parker made calls to Pettine asking for help. Pettine was in retirement so he decided to come and help after those calls. Now after about a month he's taking over. Pettine didn't give the impression he wanted to be a DC anymore. So it's easy to assume Pettine won't be here in 2027. And with that the Cowboys most likely will have their sixth different DC in six years in 2027," Calvin Watkins posted on X.

He was with Rex Ryan in Baltimore in the early to mid-2000s, but followed him to the Jets in 2009. Pettine served as Ryan's defensive coordinator with the Jets from 2009-12.

Pettine and Brian Schottenheimer crossed over for three years on the Jets. When Pettine was serving as the DC, Schottenheimer served as the Jets' OC. Now they're reunited in Dallas.

The Jets haven't been to the playoffs since those two were manning the coordinator positions for the team. Gang Green has missed the postseason in 15 consecutive years, and they appear destined for a 16th season in 2026 with their 2-8 record.

Arizona Cardinals quarterback Carson Beck (19) warms up before an NFL football game against the Los Angeles Rams in Glendale, Ariz., Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. Getty Images

#2873. Dylan Harper avoids serious hip injury in relief to Spurs

Dylan Harper's feet went out from under him with a little more than a minute left, and for a few long minutes it looked like the San Antonio Spurs' red-hot start might go down with him.

Harper has a left hip rotator strain and avoided a major injury, both ESPN and the San Antonio Express-News reported. He is expected to miss from one to three weeks.

The second-year guard got hurt late in the Spurs' 105-93 loss to the Detroit Pistons on Nov. 17. With 1:07 left, Harper stopped to plant on a pass from Stephen Castle, and his feet slid out from under him on what appeared to be a wet spot on the court. Detroit's Ausar Thompson picked off the pass while Harper stayed on the floor, grabbing at his groin.

He was down for about a minute before a Spurs foul stopped play. Teammates and staff had to help him off the court. He had 20 points, four rebounds and two assists in 35 minutes before he left.

The loss ended San Antonio's 12-game winning streak. The way the Spurs talked after the game, they seemed braced for a long absence. Two days later, the mood has changed. The San Antonio Express News reported that Harper walked into the shootaround before Thursday's game against the Los Angeles Clippers without a limp.

The Spurs had already ruled out Harper for the Nov. 19 game against the Clippers because of left hip soreness. It's the first game he has missed this season. He had started and played in all 15 games alongside De'Aaron Fox and Castle in the backcourt.

The Spurs are 12-3 and second in the Western Conference, and Harper has been a big reason why. The No. 2 pick in the 2025 NBA Draft is averaging 18.4 points, 5.4 rebounds and 4.5 assists while shooting 50.5% from the field.

This comes as a dose of good news. The Spurs are talented and deep enough to remain competitive in Harper's absence, especially because of San Antonio's depth at guard.

Harper, 20, had been in the middle of a breakout season, his first as a starter, and has become one of the more promising young players in the NBA.

He excels in transition and does not shy away from getting to the rim, which helps open up space for San Antonio's perimeter shooters, so it's not like his absence won't be felt.

But the Spurs can actually return to the starting lineup that led them to an NBA Finals appearance last season, with Julian Champagnie sliding into small forward to replace Harper.

If Harper remains sidelined past the return of Tobias Harris (calf), that would give San Antonio further flexibility, as Harris is a floor-spacing forward known for his perimeter shooting. Harris is yet to make his Spurs debut, but he would mitigate the loss of Harper, albeit in a very different way.

And while the Spurs love the contributions of Keldon Johnson, they prefer him to come off the bench as the sixth man; don't expect that to change.

All to say: San Antonio should be breathing a sigh of relief because the continued development of Harper is necessary for any championship run.

#2872. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2871. Steve Kerr completely shuts down idea of benching one Warriors star, ‘he’s our best’

The Golden State Warriors are suddenly on a terrible run in the NBA.

Steve Kerr's side went on an eight-game winning streak after their slow start to the season, but they lost Steph Curry to an injury then.

The Warriors have lost five games in a row since, the most recent of which came against the Memphis Grizzlies on Thursday.

Despite the poor run, Kerr is not changing his stance on one player.

Draymond Green has come under a fair bit of criticism for his performances recently.

The 36-year-old is among the most experienced players on the Golden State Warriors roster, and he is respected by everyone behind the scenes.

However, Green's recent outings in the Warriors' defeats have caused a debate online, with some claiming that Kerr should not start him anymore.

However, the Golden State head coach has dismissed that suggestion and claimed that he hasn't even thought about it because he doesn't have a better defensive player.

He told Luca Evans when asked if he had considered using Green as a bench option: "No, no. He's our best defensive player. And that's not even a thought."

Green missed the defeat to the Memphis Grizzlies due to right elbow injury management, but he played the Warriors' previous four games – all ended in losses.

In those fixtures, the veteran averaged four points, 4.3 rebounds, 4.3 assists, 0.8 steals and one block, which is a really poor return.

An argument can easily be made for Green to be left out of the starting five, but Kerr fully trusts him to keep him in the side, and we don't expect that to change anytime soon.

However, Green needs to take more responsibility as a senior player and step his game up, especially with Curry unavailable to bail the Warriors out.

'He's going to have to leave' – Shannon Sharpe says Stephen Curry has clear Warriors choice to make

#2870. Brock Purdy’s embarrassing viral moment resurfaces with Vikings twist

Brock Purdy has become one of the NFL's most efficient quarterbacks thanks to his ability to execute.

But that doesn't mean he hasn't had a misstep or two with the 49ers.

San Francisco 49ers quarterback Brock Purdy. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

One notable miscue resurfaced this week ahead of Purdy's return trip to Mexico City, where he last played during garbage time of a blowout win over the Cardinals in 2022 when he was Jimmy Garoppolo's backup.

The 49ers were leading by 28 points with just over seven minutes remaining, and Purdy was sent in to relieve Garoppolo and salt the game away.

On one play, Purdy took a shotgun snap and turned left to hand off the ball to running back Jordan Mason, who went to the right and forced Purdy to keep the ball. The play was botched, but Purdy wound up scrambling for a 6-yard gain while "Monday Night Football" broadcasters Joe Buck and Troy Aikman poked fun at the rookie who was the last pick in the NFL draft.

Buck noted the 49ers called Purdy, at the time, "Baby Bosa" because of his thick legs.

Aikman, laughing, added, "Baby Bosa needs to know which way to turn to hand off."

The play came up this week because Purdy was asked about it by reporters on Thursday ahead of San Francisco's trip to play the Vikings at Azteca Stadium. Oddly enough, the running back in the play, Mason, will be on the other sideline Sunday night after the 49ers traded him to the Vikings in March 2025 for fifth- and sixth-round draft picks.

Jordan Mason #27 of the Minnesota Vikings runs the ball against the Green Bay Packers in the fourth quarter of a game at U.S. Bank Stadium on Sunday, September 13, 2026, in Minneapolis, Minnesota. Getty Images

"It's funny now," Purdy said Thursday. "In the moment when I'm a rookie, I'm trying to prove myself to Kyle [Shanahan] and my teammates. So I was stressing over it after that. But it's funny that I got an opportunity after that, and we went and had success."

That game was Purdy's third career NFL appearance. He didn't throw a pass as the 49ers ran the ball to kill the clock in the 38-10 win.

But history changed two weeks after that gaffe when Garoppolo injured his foot against the Dolphins, forcing Purdy to become the team's starting quarterback. He completed 25 of 37 for 210 yards with a touchdown and interception in relief helping the 49ers win, 33-17.

Purdy went on to win his next five starts and two playoff games against the Seahawks and Cowboys before suffering a torn UCL in his throwing elbow early in the NFC Championship game against the Eagles.

The 49ers lost that game but made it to the Super Bowl the next season with Purdy under center, and he hasn't been called "Baby Bosa" since.

#2869. Gold's bad year is getting worse. Why its price keeps falling

Precious metals investors can be forgiven if they feel like the past year has given them whiplash.

Following a torrid run, which saw the price of gold hit new records 53 times in 2025, gold hit a historic high of $5,589.38 an ounce on Jan. 28, only to take an almost equally historic nosedive. On Wednesday, gold fell below $3,800 for the first time since September 2025, roughly a third below its peak.

A convergence of factors pushed gold to historic highs earlier this year, according to Suki Cooper, global head of commodities research at Standard Chartered, the London-based multinational bank. Now a different convergence is bringing the price down.

"Gold can behave as a commodity or a currency," Cooper said.

Which role it plays depends on the factors dominating the market and the psyche of investors, and right now, it's the currency argument that is bringing the precious metal down.

Cooper said gold's climb began with strong physical demand that started in 2022 and raised the floor under prices. A second phase followed as investors rethought gold's place in their portfolios. With the cost of holding a non-yielding asset falling, they started thinking about the metal for the longer term. That, Cooper said, marked a turnaround.

Until early 2025, Cooper said, long-term investors, especially those in gold-backed exchange-traded funds, had been cutting their exposure, which effectively added supply to the market. Last year, that reversed: ETF holdings, led by the SPDR Gold Shares (GLD), rose by roughly 800 tons and helped absorb the market's excess supply.

"There was a lot of one-way interest, a lot of appetite to buy but not to sell," Cooper said.

With selling in the secondary market staying muted, prices kept rising. By the end of 2025, net ETF holdings had passed 4,000 tons. Globally, gold ETFs had their strongest year ever for inflows, $89 billion, led by North America, according to the World Gold Council, with assets more than doubling to $559 billion in total.

A weaker dollar, lower bond yields and worries about global debt "set the foundation for gold to hit highs in 2026," Cooper said.

Investors who had been under-exposed to the metal kept buying.

Evan Mills, a financial advisor with Scholar Financial Advising, a fee-only firm in Winston-Salem, N.C., said the lower interest rates, expectations of further rate cuts and a weakening dollar, were all reasons for investors to favor gold.

"It was a perfect spot to be in if you were an investor in gold and commodities as a whole," Mills said. And investors did extremely well, especially with lower interest rates. "Cash doesn't do well in that environment, so a lot of people looked toward other places to hold their cash and maintain purchasing power," Mills said.

The speed of the rally made some investors uneasy, and there was growing talk that the Fed could tighten. Then, Cooper noted, the Middle East conflict broke out and gold changed roles. It stopped acting as a portfolio safe haven and became "a liquidity provider."

Cooper explained that because it was liquid and easy to sell, investors sold it to raise cash, which set off the first correction. Since then, she said, markets have been pricing in what the Fed will do.

"In 2026, the Fed has leaned more toward higher interest rates," Mills said.

This week, the Federal Reserve Board of Governors member Christopher Waller said a 50-basis point rate hike may be necessary to "shock people."

"There are higher Treasury yields and a strengthening dollar, so the opportunity cost of holding an asset that doesn't produce income is much higher," Mills said.

Mills said that in the current situation, gold investors are missing out on an environment in which cash actually does well, with money market funds that can pay 4% to 5%.

"Cash maintains purchasing power, and you don't need to put it in an illiquid asset or one where you're relying on another person to see the value," Mills said.

From 2025 to 2026, gold didn't become any less scarce; the alternatives to owning it became more attractive.

"Stocks, bonds, the classic portfolio mix, all became a lot more attractive, so people looked at the opportunity cost and decided it wasn't worth holding gold anymore," Mills said.

This has been the case for other metals in 2026 as well, with silver plummeting from an all-time high price.

After an extraordinary rally, it's easy to start questioning gold. Mills said investors should start by remembering that expectations were extremely high. "When expectations are 20% or 30% returns in a year, then of course when returns drop, people start to panic," Mills said.

Panic shouldn't be the watchword for gold investors, but caution should remain.

"In the short term, with real yields going up and the dollar likely strengthening with future Fed hikes, I'd be cautious about gold in the overall portfolio," Mills said.

For investors, the key is to understand why gold is in your portfolio to begin with.

"If you're looking for it to be a short-term purchase, then gold likely doesn't belong in your portfolio at all," Mills said. If the metal is seen as a hedge, gold can remain a valuable part of a portfolio, but it can still be a poor investment depending on the price you pay to get in.

"If you're looking at gold to be the growth section of your portfolio, you're likely going to be disappointed," he said. "Gold is insurance, not an engine, and the mistake is buying insurance when its price has skyrocketed," he added.

For investors who bought at January's peak as a modest, long-term hedge, a sharp downturn doesn't defeat the purpose, he said. "You have nothing to really worry about, because that's what the whole goal of gold was."

Gold tends to do well when the dollar weakens or when markets and geopolitics turn uncertain, Mills said, and when those pressures fade, the rest of a portfolio should pick up the slack.

"That's the whole point of insurance, and the whole point of diversification," he said. "Gold does well when the other parts of your portfolio might be slacking, and when they flip-flop, that's a good thing."

Other commodities experts say gold remains a good long-term bet. Amy Gower, head of metals and mining strategy at Morgan Stanley, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" that there are "still lots of reasons" for investors to hang on to their gold despite the correction this year.

Philip Diehl, former director of the U.S. Mint and now president of U.S. Money Reserve, an Austin, Texas-based distributor of government-issued gold, silver and platinum coins, said the long-term case for gold is still in place, but the combination of abrupt shifts in U.S. trade policy and geopolitical alliances, along with the Iran war, qualifies as a black swan event for the precious metal.

"The fundamentals that drove that bull market remain in place. They were interrupted by the Cat 5 hurricane that was unleashed in the Persian Gulf in an unwise war," Diehl said.

He expects that to change after American voters "issued a mandate" to end that war two weeks ago.

"I'm confident gold prices will return to their upward trajectory in 2027 as oil prices normalize and prices fall at the pump, allowing interest rates to stabilize," Diehl said.

Central banks continue to buy gold, with the highest monthly total in September, according to the World Gold Council, though overall purchasing is 7% lower than the same period last year.

Marc Chandler, managing partner and chief market strategist at Bannockburn Capital Markets, said surveys suggest that, in aggregate, that will not change. "However, don't only think about China or U.S. rivals," Chandler said, noting that the World Gold Council has identified Poland, a NATO member, as one of the larger buyers for most of this year.

"Retail has been burned," Chandler said. "Higher interest rates have made non-interest-bearing assets less attractive, including bitcoin."

Chandler said that the price of gold is headed to $3,500 to $3,600. Gold hit a fresh low below $3,700 on Thursday morning.

"There is nothing on the charts that says a low is in place," Chandler said. "For the anxious bottom pickers, I urge caution and being patient to see some reversal pattern. For the most part, the decline looks fairly orderly."

"Gold is still very expensive compared to anything but the last two years," Chandler said, noting that gold's 10-year (120-month) moving average is about $2,127 and its 20-year (240-month) moving average is about $1,666.

#2868. What Ron Johnson's 'Ponzi scheme' label gets wrong about Social Security, experts say

A new Senate budget committee hearing on Wednesday reignited an old Social Security question: Is the program, which provides monthly benefits to millions of Americans, a Ponzi scheme?

Senate Budget Committee Chairman Ron Johnson, a Republican representing Wisconsin, called Social Security "a legal Ponzi scheme" in his opening remarks. But experts say his comments overlook how Social Security works and the real dilemma for lawmakers over its looming funding shortfall.

A Ponzi scheme is an investment fraud that uses funds from new investors to pay existing investors, according to the Securities and Exchange Commission, Johnson wrote in his chairman's hearing memo. He said that Social Security fits the description if you remove the word "fraud."

Johnson's comments followed his Wednesday morning interview on Fox Business, where he said, "At this point, there's really no way to fix Social Security."

"You can't fix it, you just shut it down and then hope that you can recover some of your money," Johnson said during the Fox Business interview.

"As far as I can tell, this is the first time in decades that a senior senator, chairman of the budget committee, said he would rather shut down Social Security than fix it," Sen. Ron Wyden, D-Ore., said during the hearing.

At the Senate hearing, Johnson said his comments during the TV interview were "imprecise" and that he was talking broadly about Ponzi schemes, not Social Security, that need to be shut down.

Johnson said he wants lawmakers to pass a law to ensure seniors' Social Security benefits are not reduced, "then roll up our sleeves and fix the real problem, which is $24 trillion of 10-year deficits."

The Congressional Budget Office has projected a $24.4 trillion cumulative federal budget deficit over the next 10 years.

Johnson proposed a bill on Wednesday that would allow for Social Security retirement benefits to be funded by general revenue once that trust fund reaches its insolvency date.

Critics say that's not a viable solution. Using the general fund to pay for Social Security would "absolutely explode the national debt" and cost 165% of gross domestic product over 75 years, Maya MacGuineas, president of the Committee for a Responsible Federal Budget, a nonpartisan organization, said in a statement.

The way Johnson described the program during the hearing was "out of touch with the way the American public understands and knows Social Security," said Bill Sweeney, senior vice president of government affairs at AARP, a nonprofit, nonpartisan organization representing Americans age 50 and over.

"The budget committee should know that Social Security is not part of the budget," Sweeney said. "It runs on its own budget."

Social Security does affect the unified federal deficit and debt held by the public when outlays exceed dedicated revenues, according to CBO. The Treasury securities held by Social Security's trust funds count as intragovernmental debt, which is included in gross federal debt but not in debt held by the public.

Social Security benefits are mainly funded by payroll tax contributions. When the program's income exceeds its costs, the surplus is invested in special U.S. Treasury securities held in the trust funds.

Social Security is not allowed to borrow money and cannot go into debt, according to Sweeney.

"It's never contributed a penny to the debt," Sweeney said. "It's not part of the national debt because it's in a separate budget."

Social Security's trust funds may be depleted within the next six to eight years, based on current projections. If that happens, Social Security benefits may be cut.

In six years — the fourth quarter of 2032 — the Social Security retirement trust fund may run out, Social Security's trustees projected in June. At that time, 78% of those benefits would be payable.

In eight years — the third quarter of 2034 — Social Security's combined retirement and disability trust funds may run out, when 83% of scheduled benefits would be payable, according to the Social Security trustees' projections.

Had payroll tax revenue kept up with gross domestic product over the past four decades, the program would be able to pay full benefits until 2059 — an additional 25 years versus current projections, according to recent AARP research.

At the hearing, Johnson held up a picture of the trust funds, which he said is a "four-drawer file in Parkersburg, West Virginia."

Johnson similarly mentioned the trust fund files at a July 2023 hearing. In 2005, then-President George W. Bush was shown paper evidence of Social Security's U.S. bonds during a visit to the Bureau of Public Debt in Parkersburg.

While the politicians' actions seem to suggest the trust funds aren't real, experts say they are legitimate.

"It's all about trying to scare people into thinking that Social Security is not going to be there for you when it's time for you to retire," Sweeney said. "For 91 years it has been, and there's no reason to believe it won't be."

Wednesday's Senate hearing was a "missed opportunity" for a more in-depth discussion of the challenges facing Social Security, said Jason Fichtner, a witness at the hearing and executive director of the LIMRA Retirement Income Institute, a research initiative within the insurance trade association LIMRA.

Plans such as raising or eliminating the taxable maximum for payroll contributions to Social Security may push the program's funding woes 20 or 30 years later, but would not make the program solvent for the 75-year target, according to Fichtner, a former Social Security Administration official.

Moreover, fixing Social Security would not resolve the government's larger fiscal issues, which would still leave the economy vulnerable to bond market strain, he said.

"The other issue is fear," Fichtner said. "We do see evidence now of people claiming Social Security earlier because of their fear of the trust funds running out of money or Social Security not being able to pay benefits."

A June 2025 AARP survey found 49% of respondents who had recently claimed or planned to claim Social Security benefits earlier than planned were mostly motivated by concerns the program may run out of money.

"Social Security is very effective at solving problems, and it's a thorn in the side of the federal government because they have done a horrible job managing so many aspects of our finances," said labor economist Kathryn Anne Edwards, a witness at the hearing and a senior fellow at the National Academy of Social Insurance, a provider of research and policy analysis on social insurance and economic security programs.

"But Americans don't see it that way," she said.

More than half of Social Security recipients surveyed say benefits are their main source of income, according to an August AARP poll.

Social Security reform provides an opportunity to expand the program to help meet Americans' needs, Edwards said. Adding universal paid family and medical leave to the program could help individuals and families who don't currently have access to those benefits, she said. Moreover, strengthening unemployment insurance could help ensure financial security amid the prospect of AI-related job losses, Edwards said.

Senators who were elected on Nov. 3 will be in office when the retirement trust fund depletion date hits in 2032. The next session of Congress may provide an opportunity for "serious" hearings on the future of Social Security, Sweeney said.

The Senate budget committee's Wednesday hearing followed other recent hearings on the program's future, including a June Senate finance subcommittee hearing and an August Senate finance full committee hearing.

This could have been much worse. After further testing, the San Antonio Spurs have diagnosed guard Dylan Harper with a left hip external rotator strain, a story first reported by Jeff McDonald of the San Antonio Express-News.

#2864. Netflix, Amazon, Fox to bid for 2030 men's World Cup rights; Disney out

Netflix, Amazon, YouTube and Fox are all expected to submit offers to win the U.S. media rights for the 2030 men's World Cup before today's deadline, according to people familiar with the matter — but Disney will not be putting in a bid.

Fox and NBCU's Telemundo are the incumbent rights holders. FIFA would prefer to sell both the English and Spanish rights together, to one buyer, for the 2030 World Cup, to be held in Spain, Portugal and Morocco. Media companies also have the right to bid on the 2034 World Cup, which will be held in Saudi Arabia.

CNBC previously reported that media executives are planning on the winning bid coming in around $1.5 billion — or maybe even higher — although that amount could vary somewhat depending on whether both 2030 and 2034 are acquired.

FIFA is open to the idea of a potential partnership bid in which rights are split between a traditional broadcaster and a streamer, to maximize reach. Still, unlike in other countries where there are government mandates on airing games for free, FIFA will not mandate that a subscription streamer sublicense games. That decision will be up to the winning bidder.

FIFA is satisfied that enough people in the U.S. already have a Netflix or Amazon Prime Video subscription that reach won't be an issue, according to people familiar with the matter. The 2027 Women's World Cup will be on Netflix in the U.S., and Netflix plans to air all of the games without a partner.

Disney/ESPN will not be bidding, according to people familiar with the matter. ESPN Chairman Jimmy Pitaro said he was interested in World Cup rights earlier this year at CNBC Sport x Boardroom's Game Plan Summit.

In recent weeks, ESPN ran the numbers and couldn't get to $1.5 billion or higher while also having to save money for NHL, MLB and European soccer — English Premier League and Champions League — all of which will likely renew their media rights deals in 2027, said the people, who asked not to be named because the discussions were private. ESPN looked into entering a bid with a partner but ultimately decided not to bid, one of the people said.

NBCUniversal still hasn't made a decision on bidding, potentially putting Telemundo in jeopardy of losing the games for 2030, according to a person familiar with the matter. NBCUniversal has already spent billions on NBA and also needs to save money for both MLB and EPL potentially as well as the NFL, which will likely be a 2028 negotiation.

Skydance and Apple will not bid, according to people familiar with the matter.

A FIFA spokesperson declined to comment, citing confidentiality of the tender process.

FIFA executives plan to travel to the U.S. next week to meet personally with bidders to assess their bids and potentially get final offers. FIFA plans to choose a winner before the end of the year.

#2862. RJ Luis Jr. will ‘stand down’ and stop playing at LSU, his lawyer says

BATON ROUGE, La. — RJ Luis Jr.'s fight to play basketball at LSU appears to be over.

The attorney for the St. John's transfer told ESPN late Thursday night that Luis will "stand down" rather than risk damage to the university.

"He said, 'Tony, I don't want to do anything that will hurt my teammates, my coach or LSU,'" attorney Tony Clayton told the network. "What he was saying was, in essence, that the juice is not worth the squeeze."

Luis' decision came hours after LSU president Wade Rousse recommended that coach Will Wade have Luis sit out amid pushback from the Southeastern Conference and the NCAA.

Rousse said in a statement on Thursday that he has "instructed" Wade not to play Luis. He made the suggestion less than 24 hours after Luis had 11 and nine rebounds in No. 25 LSU's 79-67 win over Louisiana-Monroe.

Luis, the 2025 Big East Player of the Year while at St. John's, spent a brief period with the Utah Jazz and Boston Celtics after not being selected in the NBA draft before joining the Tigers.

The NCAA and the SEC have argued in court that Luis is ineligible because he chose to leave college to enter the NBA draft, never withdrew from the draft, and spent time with two professional teams.

A Louisiana state district court judge in Baton Rouge made it possible for Luis to play in college again when he issued a temporary injunction against the NCAA and the conference.

Luis was not made available to reporters after making his debut, and Wade declined to get into specifics on why Luis was on the roster other than to say Luis was eligible, so he played.

Elliot Abrams, a Raleigh, North Carolina, lawyer who is part of the legal team representing Luis, called the threats made by the SEC to expel an institution for playing an athlete who has been declared eligible "reprehensible."null

Abrams added that it "seriously harms a young person who was caught in the middle of a power struggle having nothing to do with him."

Clayton told ESPN that basketball is still in Luis' future but declined to speculate on potential next steps.

#2861. Could Gennings Dunker’s return revive the Steelers’ jumbo package?

Despite being a third-round pick in the 2026 NFL Draft, Iowa tackle-turned-guard Gennings Dunker seemed destined for a redshirt rookie season with the Steelers.

Dunker was buried on the depth chart over the summer, missing some time with injury and then opening the regular season as a gameday inactive. Then, in late September, he landed on injured reserve with a reported MCL sprain.

Pittsburgh opened Dunker's 21-day return window from IR on Wednesday, the same day the team placed starting left guard Mason McCormick, who is expected to sit out until the playoffs, on the injured list. And although the Steelers' coaching staff has said the usual platitudes regarding Dunker's development so far this season, the rookie's lack of experience thus far makes it clear that backup lineman Spencer Anderson will be the new starting left guard for the immediate future.

Of course, there's a chance Anderson underperforms or Dunker impresses enough over the next few weeks for the rookie to overtake the veteran in the starting lineup. But whether or not that does happen, Dunker could still play a notable role for the Steelers as the sixth lineman in the offense's jumbo package.

The Steelers started using their jumbo package this season in Week 7, a 16-10 win over the Saints where Pittsburgh rushed for 148 yards. It appeared again in Week 8's victory over the Cleveland Browns, where the Steelers managed 200 yards on the ground.

Anderson, of course, has served as that sixth lineman in the jumbo package for the Steelers in recent seasons, with Pittsburgh making the package part of its offensive identity in 2025 under then-coordinator Arthur Smith. New head coach Mike McCarthy made the choice to emphasize it once again midway through 2026, and the results were still overwhelmingly positive.

According to Sumer Sports, Pittsburgh averages +0.25 EPA per rush when using its jumbo package in 11 personnel this season, well above the team's still-good +0.06 EPA per rush across all personnel. Per a detailed breakdown from BTSC's Ryan Parish following the Browns game, the Steelers earned a first down or touchdown on 37.5% of their runs out of a six-lineman package in Weeks 7-8.

Why did McCarthy take so long to come back to the jumbo package? A number of reasons could be in play, including game-by-game matchups and Pittsburgh's rash of wide receiver injuries in recent weeks. However, McCarthy also hinted after the Saints game that the team didn't use the package early as Anderson began the season in the starting lineup before suffering a reported bone chip injury in late September.

Once Brock Hoffman took over as a starting guard and Anderson was healthy again, the latter was available to play as the sixth lineman and the jumbo package suddenly reappeared.

With Anderson about to go back into the starting lineup, it might not be a great sign for the future of the jumbo package. But Dunker might be able to fill that role.

The Steelers seem to like Anderson as the sixth lineman because his athleticism (9.35 RAS as a guard) and versatility make him a strong candidate for the almost pseudo-tight end nature position.

The Steelers didn't seem to like Hoffman in that role while Anderson was the starter as the former scored just a 4.19 RAS at guard and has largely been an interior player over his career. Those same concerns shouldn't apply as much to Dunker, who has a 9.61 RAS as a guard after playing tackle during his Iowa days. And while Dunker is still learning as a pass blocker, his road-grading ability in the run game should translate to the pros more immediately.

Beyond that, Dunker's mean demeanor in the ground game fits perfectly with the bully-ball identity of the Steelers' jumbo package. Despite Pittsburgh moving Anderson back into the starting lineup following McCormick's injury, the pending activation of Dunker might not spell the end to one of the Steelers' most successful offensive packages this season.

And facing a bottom-10 rushing defense in the Philadelphia Eagles in Week 11, Sunday's game could be a good place to start.

#2860. As Sanofi's Pompe drug shortage drags on, some patients struggle to access treatments

Two weeks ago, Monique Griffin got a troubling email.

The French drugmaker Sanofi told her that a shipping delay had interrupted supply of the drug that she relies on to manage her rare muscle-wasting disease. Her next infusion was canceled. Griffin, who is 51 and uses a wheelchair due to Pompe disease, fears what will happen if the shortage isn't resolved soon.

"You're basically letting the disease take over again," she said. "It's terrifying."

More than three months after initial reports of supply problems with treatments for Pompe disease, the disruption drags on, with more patients learning their infusions have been canceled. Sanofi said it has made progress restoring the supply to patients in some countries, but added that shipping delays could interrupt infusions for some U.S. patients through the end of November.

For more stories like this, sign up for the CNBC Cures newsletter.

A Sanofi spokesperson said there were no issues with drug quality and the company is "committed to ensuring that the most vulnerable patients receive treatment first during this period." The company added that patients should speak with a health professional before taking another treatment, adding, "these medicines are not interchangeable."

In a statement, the Food and Drug Administration said "patient access to these treatments is a priority for the agency and we have offered our assistance to Sanofi on anything needed to help ensure all patient needs are met."

About one in every 19,000 babies is born with Pompe disease, which is named after the Dutch pathologist who discovered the condition nearly a century ago.

Patients lack an enzyme for breaking down a complex sugar, causing a toxic buildup of glycogen inside their cells. Their muscles weaken and their heart can become enlarged. They have trouble breathing and eating. Left untreated, the disease is often fatal.

Sanofi's Lumizyme and a second-generation version, Nexviazyme, replace the missing enzyme, protecting the patient's muscles. Patients typically receive Sanofi's drugs every two weeks.

"It's a lifeline," said Jenna Yarborough, a rare disease advocate whose two children with Pompe disease have not been affected by the drug shortage.

While Pompe disease patients rely on Sanofi's drugs, several other treatments have entered clinical trials, including separate gene therapies from AskBio and Astellas Pharma.

Sanofi says the drug shortage is the result of issues at its manufacturing facility in Ireland, which public documents show the company has known about for several months. The FDA inspected the plant between January 12 and January 20, 2026 and found violations involving two unrelated drugs. The company began responding to the violations in February, but on June 22, the FDA sent the company a warning letter saying it had still not addressed the "underlying failure" to comply with manufacturing requirements. Addressing the FDA's ongoing concerns has contributed to the supply disruption of the Pompe therapies, according to Sanofi.

There's a sense of frustration within the Pompe community that the company did not do enough to warn patients about the potential for a supply disruption. Ryan Colburn, a person living with Pompe who is active in the advocacy space, says many patients were unaware of the manufacturing issues at Sanofi and were left with no way to prepare for them. "The lack of proactive transparency and apparent lack of buffer are of huge concern for any medicine that people's lives and well-being depend on access to," Colburn said.

A spokesperson for Sanofi responded, "As the situation evolved, and the extent of the disruption became clearer, we engaged global and U.S. patient advocacy organizations, patients and clinicians to inform them, and we have worked to continue to keep these groups and individuals updated through regular communications. We remain committed to continuity of care and to transparent, ongoing communication with the Pompe community as we work to stabilize supply."

Chelsey McGee's 10-year-old son Logan has taken Lumizyme every two weeks since he was diagnosed with Pompe disease as an infant. While his infusions haven't been canceled yet, she's concerned that missing even one appointment will damage his heart. Sanofi's drug has kept him healthy enough to play soccer.

"It's important that we stay on his timeline and keep his heart healthy," she said.

McGee, who lives in Minneapolis, is frustrated by what she says is a lack of transparency from Sanofi. She discovered the FDA cited the pharmaceutical company for manufacturing problems by searching the agency's website, not from the company.

Griffin, who lives in Orlando, has taken Lumizyme since 2010 when she was diagnosed with an adult-onset version of the disease. The diagnosis came after she became so fatigued that she fell while carrying groceries and suffered a concussion.

She knows what it's like to go without her infusions. She missed a few appointments earlier this year due to an insurance mix-up. She likened it to having "a really bad flu." She became extremely fatigued, struggled to breathe and started to feel pain in her legs, arms and back.

Pompe is a progressive disease, meaning it gets worse over time. She worries most about losing the strength to breathe. She sleeps with assisted ventilation.

She works in the theme parks industry and is hoping to attend a conference this week. While her Lumizyme infusions remain on hold, she hopes to get through the week of meetings "on caffeine and goodwill."

CNBC Cures is underwritten by Alexion, AstraZeneca Rare Disease and BridgeBio.

#2859. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2857. 3-step method to influence people and win them over, from a Stanford expert: ‘Your voice is like a muscle’

Think back on the most memorable, inspiring speeches or presentations you've witnessed. They probably weren't delivered in a monotone, Matt Abrahams says.

"The human brain is designed to pick out and focus on things that are novel and new," says Abrahams, a Stanford University lecturer on strategic communication and host of the "Think Fast, Talk Smart" podcast. "So anything that stays the same, like a monotonous voice or cadence, we don't pay as much attention to."

Indeed, successful leaders often have an uncanny ability to win over a crowd: The power of persuasion lies not just in what you actually have to say, but also in how you say it, research shows. The topic has taken on new relevance this week as Nvidia interim CEO Colette Kress steps into one of the most closely watched leadership roles in corporate America.

In appearances this week, including Nvidia's quarterly earnings call and an interview with CNBC's "Mad Money" host Jim Cramer, Kress has discussed the company's performance. She's also addressed employees and investors in a time of leadership upheaval, following Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang's high-profile resignation on Nov. 12.

How Kress communicates has drawn attention and, in some circles, criticism. On Wednesday, The Wall Street Journal described her public speaking style as "slow, steady, almost monotone." That doesn't necessarily make it ineffective. Her situation, however, highlights a broader challenge leaders face when they need to inform, reassure and persuade large groups of people at once.

Generally speaking, anyone can stand a better chance at persuading others to follow them by honing an engaging speaking style, says Abrahams. Some people may be naturally expressive and engaging. For others, it's a skill that requires time and practice.

Abrahams suggests a three-step playbook for anyone learning to speak more persuasively: "repetition, reflection and feedback."

Abrahams' simplest advice for making yourself a more engaging speaker: "When you're using emotive words, reflect the emotion in them." That means adding some emphasis to words like "exciting," "huge" or "challenging," and so on. If you tend to speak monotonously, leaning into obviously emotive words can help you vary your tone and inflection enough to keep your audience's attention.

There's a catch, of course: People find inauthenticity off-putting and disingenuous, says Abrahams. Don't alter your voice entirely, he suggests — just add some variety to your intonation and speaking volume. Learning to do so while still sounding natural takes practice, he notes.

"Your voice is like a muscle," says Abrahams. "You can train it. You can grow it [and] strengthen it, just like you go to the gym to lift weights."

Practicing repetition starts with reading aloud from a chosen text, which Abrahams recommends so you don't have to overthink what you're saying. And the same way you might lift heavy weights to get stronger, Abrahams suggests reading overly expressive material to start getting out of your comfort zone and practice being energetic and engaging. He instructs his Stanford students to try reading Dr. Seuss books, as their playful words and bouncy rhymes can naturally make for a lively performance.

While practicing reading out loud, record yourself to watch and listen to later, Abrahams recommends. Then, use the recording to reflect on what works and what doesn't, and incorporate your self-feedback into your next reading. You can also play the recording for a few trusted peers to get their thoughts, Abrahams says.

If a line reading doesn't sound natural in your voice, feels flat or even rushed, keep trying it until you're satisfied, he says. Leaders are often asked to deliver lengthy speeches or talk for long periods in interviews and panels, so it's important to build your vocal stamina, he adds.

"We have to calibrate ourselves," he says.

Abrahams asks his Stanford graduate students to review their recordings in three ways, he says:

"That's how you train your brain to see: 'What do I need to sound like?'" says Abrahams.

#2855. LeBron James sues makers of 'Bron' app over unauthorized use of his nickname and AI-generated likeness

Former Lakers star LeBron James is suing a company and its founders for using his nickname as the name of a sports-betting analytics app and using unauthorized AI-generated images in his likeness to advertise the product.

According to a civil lawsuit filed Tuesday in U.S. District Court of New York by James and his LBJ Trademarks LLC, the San Francisco-based developer PlayVision Inc. named its app "Bron" in an attempt to benefit from James' name and star power "without any connection between the word and Defendants or any feature of their app."

"The word 'Bron' has no dictionary definition and no special meaning in the context of sports analytics, wagering, or artificial intelligence," the complaint states. "Rather, in the context of anything sports-related, it refers to only one man — LeBron James."

Read more: LeBron James says 'family is safe' after arrest of alleged stalker outside Brentwood home

The Times on Thursday reached out to PlayVision and fellow defendants Anish Gupta (co-founder and CEO) and Marc Zoghby (co-founder and CTO) through the company's email address and did not immediately receive a response. The Associated Press reports that requests for comment sent Tuesday to Gupta and Zoghby's email accounts were also not immediately returned.

In early November, PlayVision released a video advertisement for the Bron app that featured a player "with physical traits that mimic Mr. James, down to his identifiable tattoos," according to the complaint,. The player is shown mainly from behind, with a partial side view of his face also displayed at one point, while using the app.

He wears a basketball jersey that features the name "Bron" and the number 23, which is the jersey number James has worn during most of his 24 years in the NBA (including his current stint with the Philadelphia 76ers).

Read more: Swanson: One frigid night against the Jazz doesn't change the fact the Lakers are legit

"The Ad catapulted the Bron App from obscurity to controversy, with the Ad gaining a million views within a day—exactly as Defendants intended," the lawsuit states, adding, "When Mr. James's counsel sent a cease-and-desist letter to Defendants, they used it as an opportunity to gin up additional publicity, taking down the Ad while teasing 'a new name' without delivering one..."

On Monday, Gupta posted a video to his LinkedIn page that featured clips of people discussing and, at one point, chanting "Bron AI." The post promoted the relaunch of the Bron app under a new name later in the week.

"We found Bron. Apparently, the lawyers found it too," Gupta wrote. "Find out our new name Wednesday. 10 AM PT."

The new app's name, which was introduced on the same day as James' lawsuit was filed, is "Evie."

Read more: Stephen Curry calls out Gilbert Arenas, others over comments about wife Ayesha. Here's the latest

"Defendants' latest post underscores that Defendants' entire marketing campaign has been in bad faith, referencing the public backlash against their Ad and daring Mr. James to sue," the complaint states. "Defendants are not winding down their misconduct. They are milking it, stringing out the controversy one video teaser at a time for as long as it keeps the Bron App in the headlines."

#2854. Kraken parent's acquisition of Tribe Capital flags new chapter in evolution of crypto exchanges

Payward, the owner of crypto exchange Kraken, is pushing into private markets with its latest acquisition, underscoring a broader shift in cryptocurrencies, from trading-led growth to the buildout of financial infrastructure.

Payward said late Wednesday that it's buying venture capital and asset management provider Tribe Capital Management, led by Payward's co-CEO Arjun Sethi. No purchase price was disclosed.

Tribe, where Sethi is co-founder and chairman, invests across early- and late-stage private companies and has $2.7 billion in assets under management.

"Joining Payward gives us the opportunity to bring Tribe's investment approach to a global financial platform," Jonathan Hsu, another co-founder of Tribe Capital, said in a statement. "We see a strong fit between our investment approach and Payward's infrastructure, distribution, and product-building capabilities."

Payward didn't respond to a request for comment.

The acquisition is novel among crypto exchanges. Unlike exchange-owned venture arms such as Coinbase Ventures or Binance Labs, Payward is looking to integrate Tribe's investment and fund-management capabilities into an existing custody, trading, collateral and tokenization infrastructure to develop private-market investment products.

Payward is trying to position itself to capture value from a broader crypto-driven transformation of financial services, not use the venture vehicle to draw more capital into the crypto ecosystem.

The acquisition is another step in the evolution in the crypto exchange business model. With cryptocurrency prices continuing to decline this year, crypto exchanges have worked to move past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, focusing instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance.
