#2830. Indiana’s Curt Cignetti Uses Clown Emoji to Blast SEC Media Writers: “Fake News”

BLOOMINGTON, INDIANA - OCTOBER 31: Indiana University President Pamela Whitten congratulates Curt Cignetti, head coach of the Indiana Hoosiers after the game against the Minnesota Golden Gophers at Memorial Stadium on October 31, 2026 in Bloomington, Indiana. (Photo by Michael Hickey/Getty Images) Getty

The Indiana Hoosiers' head coach, Curt Cignetti, took a shot at SEC media writers on Thursday night. After a report connected him to a potential coaching opening at Michigan, Cignetti responded on social media with a rejection. He also included a clown emoji, leaving little doubt about his opinion of the speculation.

"Fake news created by three SEC media writers. Nice try [clown emoji]," Cignetti wrote on X, adding the emoji.

The response puts his position on the record at a time when coaching speculation is picking up. For Indiana fans wondering whether their coach could entertain a move within the Big Ten, his reaction offered reassurance. It also gave Michigan fans a reason to reconsider one name being discussed.

The CBS Sports report came from John Talty, Richard Johnson and Chris Hummer. It discussed potential candidates if Michigan were to move on from Kyle Whittingham, including Cignetti. That distinction matters because the discussion concerned a possible opening, rather than an announced vacancy.

The report also raised questions about Indiana's resources and the outlook for its 2027 roster. Its premise was that another program with greater financial resources could offer a different opportunity. Cignetti challenged the reporting with his post rather than leaving that speculation unanswered.

GettyBLOOMINGTON, INDIANA – OCTOBER 31: Indiana University President Pamela Whitten congratulates Curt Cignetti, head coach of the Indiana Hoosiers after the game against the Minnesota Golden Gophers at Memorial Stadium on October 31, 2026 in Bloomington, Indiana. (Photo by Michael Hickey/Getty Images)

His reference to "SEC media writers" made the response harsher and personal. Instead of issuing a standard statement about focusing on the next game, he went directly at the people responsible for the report. The clown emoji added another layer to that criticism without requiring another sentence.

There is context beyond Thursday's exchange. Cignetti has previously said he plans to retire at Indiana, and his contract runs through 2033. Those details matter when evaluating speculation about whether he wants to coach elsewhere.

Local reporting also challenged the Michigan connection. Michael Niziolek of The Herald-Times reported that a source told the Indianapolis Star the idea of Cignetti leaving for Michigan was "100% false." That account preceded Cignetti's public response.

For Hoosiers fans, the useful takeaway is straightforward: their coach publicly disputed the story connecting him to Michigan. His post does not answer every question about future roster construction or program funding. It does make clear that he rejected this portrayal of his situation.

Coaching rumors rarely disappear after one social media post. Cignetti nevertheless made sure Indiana supporters heard his response directly from him. In this case, it's neat for college football fans to hear directly from the head coach. Right on his personal social media account. That is 2026 journalism at its finest.

#2828. Kyren Williams hopes GTA VI release distracts other NFL teams

#2827. ‘It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround

'It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers created their two cleanest late-game looks several hours before they needed them.

During Wednesday morning's shootaround in Orlando, Nick Nurse had the Sixers work on offensive material they had not consistently used this season. They tested one of those actions during the first half, returned to it throughout the closing minutes and watched it produce the shots they needed to complete a 17-point comeback.

"We worked on some stuff this morning that we hadn't used before, and they ran all that stuff very, very well and very calmly and with composure," Nurse told reporters.

The Sixers have earned six of their 13 wins after trailing by double digits. They are also 11-0 in games that reached clutch time.

Relying on comebacks is not a sustainable plan. The repeated late-game situations, however, have forced a new roster to respond to different lineups, coverages and responsibilities while the season is still young.

Each close finish gives Nurse more information about which players and actions hold up when possessions tighten. Wednesday gave him a new option.

Anfernee Simons' clinching three in the Sixers' 121-116 win over the Magic was the final result of that preparation.

"That last play, we ran that play in the first half," Simons said. "We worked it a couple of times, and in the second half we ran that play every single time down the stretch, and it worked every time.

"It was a play we ran through. [It's gratifying] especially when it's working consistently and you're getting different shots out of it and the opposing team just don't know what to do.

"We just continue to run the play and get the open shots we need."

NBC Sports Philadelphia video producer and Sixers Talk podcast co-host Kevin Rice broke down how the Sixers used its different options to keep Orlando guessing.

The action begins with two players stationed near the elbows. A player on the opposite elbow then sets a screen for the player up top, who receives the ball while moving around it.

The Sixers ran the set three consecutive times during the first half. None of the possessions reached the primary option. Each still produced a quality opportunity.

On the first, Joel Embiid cleared LeBron James out of the area and directed Simons around him for a dribble handoff. Simons received an open three-point look.

Orlando took away the handoff the next time down but left Embiid one-on-one against Wendell Carter Jr. Embiid created enough room to retrieve his own miss and convert an and-one opportunity.

The third attempt pulled Orlando's defense into the paint. Two passes moved the ball through the collapsing coverage and created an open three for VJ Edgecombe.

"We had a little thing where he comes flying off behind Joel, and he doesn't get to do that that much, right?" Nurse said of Simons. "So tonight he gets the opportunity to do it, and it was very good for us on a number of plays."

The Sixers changed the action late without abandoning its initial Horns alignment. Simons moved through the lane to help form a Spain pick-and-roll involving James and Embiid.

On the first late possession, Simons remained near the play after James gained a matchup against Carter. Orlando struggled to sort out its assignments, leaving James open for a 19-foot jumper with 54.8 seconds remaining.

After Jalen Suggs made one of two free throws, the Sixers returned to the same setup. This time, Simons relocated to the left corner after James drew Carter on a switch.

James drove into the paint. Suggs lost track of Simons while responding to the penetration, and James sent the ball toward the corner.

Simons buried the open three to give the Sixers a 119-113 lead with 15 seconds left.

"Bron just penetrated the paint," Simons said. "It kind of got some confusion, and when I was on the dunker and kind of spaced out to the corner, I ended up finding myself wide open for the three, and Bron found me."

The Sixers entered the night without Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown, their two leading scorers. Simons made his first start with Philadelphia and recorded 18 points in the city where he grew up.

His final basket also illustrated why Nurse believes the Sixers have more to uncover with him.

"When he's out there, they gotta be really careful," Nurse said. "So I think there's some drives he creates, and just about every time he lets it go, whether he's on balance or not, you think it's going in, and that is a big deal. Like, that is a big deal.

"And again, I think we're still a ways away from really unlocking him to being even more part of some of the things we can do with him to create more shots."

Philadelphia is now 11-0 in clutch games. The finish in Orlando provided a more specific explanation than experience or shot-making alone.

The Sixers installed a set in the morning, explored its counters and trusted it when the game tightened. James supplied the patience to read Orlando's coverage. Embiid occupied the middle of the action. Simons moved without the ball, and Edgecombe remained ready to shoot.

James said his heart rate drops when close games increase the pressure on everyone else.

"I mean, experience," James said. "A guy that's seen every situation this league has to offer. It's nothing I haven't seen. And, you know, in the moments where people's heart rate goes up or anxiety goes up, mine is pretty low. It's actually lower, you know."

The Sixers still have more actions and reads to learn as Nurse becomes familiar with his new personnel, but their latest win showed their innate ability to adapt in big moments.

"I think we're still a long ways away from knowing all the reads and all that stuff because it's just a little early, right?" Nurse said.

#2825. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2824. Intuit to sell Mailchimp to Bending Spoons for $3.68 billion

Tax software maker Intuit said Thursday that it has agreed to sell marketing subsidiary Mailchimp to Italian technology consolidator Bending Spoons for $3.68 billion in cash and stock. The deal is set to close in early 2027, according to a statement.

The sale is a costly retreat for Intuit, which paid $12 billion for Mailchimp in 2021 in a push beyond its core tax and accounting businesses. Five years later, the software maker is unloading the email-marketing company for less than one-third of that price after Mailchimp became a drag on its overall revenue growth.

The deal is the latest instance of Bending Spoons picking up a once promising technology asset. The company bought productivity software startup Airtable in September for 10% of what it was once worth.

The Bending Spoons portfolio also contains AOL, Brightcove, Eventbrite, Evernote, Meetup, Vimeo, and WeTransfer.

In August Intuit started running Mailchimp as a distinct operating segment. The move was meant to give investors more transparency as the company tried to manage the unit for profitability, Sandeep Aujla, Intuit's finance chief, said at a September company briefing.

Also on Thursday, Intuit announced stronger-than-expected earnings and revenue for its fiscal first quarter, which ended on Oct. 31. The company reported $2.91 in adjusted earnings per share on $4.35 billion in revenue. Analysts polled by LSEG were looking for $2.70 per share and $4.31 billion in revenue.

While Intuit's revenue increased 12% year over year, Mailchimp revenue declined 1% to $313 million. Analysts polled by StreetAccount had expected $314.4 million.

Management called for $22.88 to $23.12 in adjusted earnings per share for the 2027 fiscal year, with $23.081 billion to $23.365 billion in revenue. Analysts polled by LSEG had predicted $23.62 in adjusted earnings per share on $23.43 billion in revenue. Intuit shares tumbled 15% in extended trading.

The company said Mailchimp should produce $1.256 billion to $1.266 billion in fiscal 2027 revenue, which would be down 1% to flat year over year. In August Aujla said Intuit anticipated Mailchimp's higher effective prices offsetting increased client churn. Mailchimp was founded in 2001. Rivals include Klaviyo, which went public in 2023.

Berkshire Hathaway invested $600 million in Bending Spoons when the acquirer went public in July, and in October Bending Spoons said Berkshire invested another $330 million.

#2822. One of the world's most contested seas is proving too much for Asia's leaders to resolve

One of the world's most contested bodies of water continues to cause problems for Asia's leaders.

A substantive Code of Conduct in the South China Sea does not seem to be feasible despite ASEAN nations expressing optimism that the negotiations could finally conclude after more than two decades, experts told CNBC.

The ASEAN summit in the Philippines failed to resolve it, despite Manila saying it intended to conclude the Code this year.

The crux of the issue is that China claims virtually all of the South China Sea as its own territory, despite international rulings saying it's not the case and historical claims from many other countries.

China's "nine-dash line" claim was ruled as invalid by the Permanent Court of Arbitration in 2016, although Beijing has refused to recognize that ruling.

Over decades, China has been building shoals into structures that strengthen its territorial assertions, and has become increasingly aggressive in policing the territory against nations such as the Philippines, that claim traditional rights over the maritime areas.

The Code of Conduct for the South China Sea aims to address areas where multiple ASEAN nations and China have disputes and competing claims.

Manila has reported that it had completed 80% of the negotiating text, with negotiations to continue under next ASEAN chair Singapore. But, experts are skeptical that a "substantive and effective" agreement can be realized, at least in the short term.

"It's an annual ritual. The ASEAN Chair commits to completing the Code of Conduct in January, but by November is reporting that — although great progress has been made — they didn't quite get there this year. Rinse, repeat," Ray Powell, executive director at gray-zone activities tracker SeaLight Foundation told CNBC.He described the 80% number as "a meaningless statistic," given that the issue at hand was complex and contentious.

ASEAN secretary general Kao Kim Hourn still sees potential for resolution. "I don't think it's a failure at all," he told CNBC's JP Ong. "There's still a lot of commitment and a lot of leadership on both sides to move ahead and get it done."

But Collin Koh, senior fellow at the Institute of Defence and Strategic Studies in Singapore's Nanyang Technological University, told CNBC that even if 80% of the text has been set, the 20% remaining contain the most persistent problems, including geographical scope, competing interests among ASEAN member states, and enforcement.

Koh said the original aim was ASEAN to negotiate with China "one-to-one," but, it has never been the case.

"It has always been 10 against one. Now you add Timor Leste. Now 11 against one. So, because of that, there is never any unified ASEAN position that they will bring to the table with with China," he pointed out.

Disputes in the South China Sea stretch at least as far back as the 1970s, with various countries such as Brunei, Indonesia, Malaysia, the Philippines and Vietnam claiming various zones and islands in the critical maritime region, which houses rich natural resources and fishing areas.

Back in 2015, Singapore has positioned itself as a "neutral broker" in ASEAN-China dialogue, with then-Prime Minister Lee Hsien Loong saying that Singapore will deal "fairly and openly with all parties" in order to "preserve peace and stability in the region."

But while the city-state may be able to broker some compromises, the disagreements that have held up the code for almost a quarter of a century will persist, according to Lynn Kuok, Lee Kuan Yew Chair of Southeast Asia Studies at the Brookings Institution.

"Beijing, moreover, has little incentive to accept meaningful restraints while it continues to consolidate its position in the South China Sea," Kuok added.

Her view was echoed by Joshua Kurlantzick, senior fellow for Southeast Asia and South Asia at the Council on Foreign Relations, who said that a code of conduct is unlikely to happen soon, if ever. "China at its heart doesn't really want a code of conduct, and it continues to push more assertively into the South China Sea." He pointed out Beijing's actions against the Philippines in the region, including around the disputed Scarborough Shoal.

Chinese coast guard vessels have most recently been accused of ramming Filipino fishing vessels near the Spratly islands in September, and have used lasers and water cannons against Filipino ships in the past.

Kurlantzick also said that there are multiple countries within ASEAN that are close to China that are willing to hold up any agreement on the Code, adding that the move toward China is also happening as the U.S.' commitment to defense in the region becomes less clear.

"I do not think there is really any chance of a code of conduct happening with Singapore as ASEAN chair — or really, anyone as ASEAN chair," he said.

At the same time, a rushed or flawed Code would be worse than none at all, Powell said."In fact, the worst outcome is not a failure to agree, but rather a weak Code that would enable Beijing to undermine UNCLOS," he said. The United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea, which came into force in 1994, provides a comprehensive legal framework for oceans and seas globally.

"The narrative from China could then be 'see, now we have this shiny new regional agreement that overrides that problematic law invented by outsiders who don't understand East Asia and shouldn't have a say here anyway,'" Powell added.

—CNBC's Joanna Ossinger contributed to this article.

#2821. Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return

Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

PG-13 was rated R in his return to Philadelphia.

The middle finger Paul George directed toward one baseline fan only reinforced that rating.

Tuesday night was expected to revolve around Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum sharing an NBA court as opponents for the first time. Instead, the other headliner in the summer's blockbuster trade stole the show.

George scored a game-high 36 points in Boston's 123-101 win over the Sixers. He shot 14 for 22 from the floor, made six of his 11 three-point attempts and scored 14 consecutive Boston points in the fourth quarter.

The game was tied at 79 with 3:19 remaining in the third quarter. Boston outscored Philadelphia 44-22 from that point forward.

The Celtics went nine minutes and 20 seconds without making a two-point field goal during the second half. Instead, they made nine consecutive field goals from three, the second-longest such streak by a Sixers opponent over the last 30 seasons.

Payton Pritchard opened the fourth with consecutive threes. George followed with eight straight points, helping the Celtics begin the quarter on a 14-0 run that turned a competitive game into a rout.

George's return carried a complicated history. His two seasons with the Sixers were repeatedly interrupted by injuries, but he also played a significant role in their most memorable playoff run in years.

Last spring, George helped the Sixers overcome a 3-1 deficit to eliminate Boston in seven games. He averaged 17.4 points and shot 55 percent from three during the series.

Tyrese Maxey was happy to see his former teammate Tuesday, even if he disliked what followed.

"It was good to see Paul," Maxey said. "I love him, love his family. … He gave us everything he had while he was here. We know what he did in the playoffs last year. Great person, great family, and we'll see him again."

Maxey then added one important qualifier.

George instead scored from nearly everywhere.

"He had some open threes. He had some pull-up threes. He had some middies," Maxey said. "He played within the game, too. That's what Paul does. He plays within the game. He takes what the defense gives him, and we gave him too many open looks tonight.

"PG, we know what PG does. We shouldn't even be allowing him to get up 11 threes, and that's on us."

Nick Nurse watched George attack the Sixers in several ways.

"He really had it going," Nurse said. "He'd post up, and-one, fallaways. He went down the lane, high archer off the glass, and-one. He scored in about every way he could tonight."

George heard boos during his return and said the arena carried the atmosphere, though not quite the intensity, of a playoff game.

"This building can be very electric as well," George said. "It was fun. It was fun to play in this environment."

One interaction extended beyond the usual reception for a returning player.

During a stoppage, the ball rolled toward a fan seated along the baseline. George said the fan looked directly at him before dropping the ball instead of returning it.

"There was a fan being an a–," George said. "The ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do.

"And so, yeah, then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

#2820. Daniel Cormier Accuses Jon Jones of Using Josh Hokit Feud to Put Himself in the Spotlight

Josh Hokit may have fallen short of becoming the UFC heavyweight champion at UFC 334, but his five-round war against Ciryl Gane earned him plenty of recognition across the MMA community. Even Dana White was impressed by the American's performance despite the disappointing result.

Jon Jones, however, had a very different reaction. Rather than acknowledge Hokit's efforts, the former heavyweight champion took the opportunity to mock him for failing to capture the title. The exchange also carries an interesting backstory, as Hokit trains under Greg Jackson at Jackson Wink MMA, the same gym where Jones spent a significant portion of his career.

Jones famously needed less than three minutes to finish Gane at UFC 285, submitting the Frenchman to claim the vacant heavyweight championship. Following UFC 334, he reminded everyone of that accomplishment by taking a dig at his former gym's latest heavyweight contender. Sharing a post on Instagram, Jones wrote, "Same coaches different results."

ALSO READ: Israel Adesanya Backs Former Teammate Navajo Stirling to Finish Jiri Prochazka at UFC Qatar

The former champion also mocked Hokit over his previous remarks about him, including the viral rap song in which the American took shots at Jones. He suggested that fighters who speak negatively about him often end up losing their next fights. What followed was an increasingly personal exchange, with both men trading insults involving Jones' controversial steroid history and matters outside the Octagon.

Their feud has now drawn criticism from Daniel Cormier, who questioned why Jones continues to involve himself in such disputes despite being retired and having no confirmed plans to compete again.

Speaking on his YouTube channel, Cormier observed Jones' tendency to resurface whenever someone he has a history with suffers a setback. He said, "Jon Jones always has something to say about these moments."

He added, "When you talk about Jon, he has this certain thing that he does where he will have issues with people, he will lay in wait, and when that person has an issue, Jon Jones will then surface and take shots at them."

Cormier then pointed to the latest exchange with Hokit, explaining how their longstanding animosity became apparent following UFC 334. He said, "That happened with Josh Hokit this weekend. Obviously, Josh Hokit and Jon Jones have a bad history, and that was never more evident than this weekend. Obviously, Hokit always goes at Jones, and then Jones has started to respond."

The former two-division champion also felt that Jones chose the wrong moment to target Hokit, considering the performance the heavyweight had just delivered against Gane.

Cormier said, "It was just over, and over, and over again. I could read this for 10 minutes. My point being, Jon tends to come when he thinks that someone that has spoken in opposition to him is down. It's hard for me to sit back and see this when you can't leave Madison Square Garden after watching that fight, regardless of the outcome, without praising Josh for what he did in that fight."

He further criticized Jones for repeatedly targeting people when they are at their lowest rather than acknowledging their achievements. Cormier said, "To me, that's why it's disappointing about the way that Jon Jones actually attacks people, and he's done it time and time again. In most cases, it's when that person's down. But in other cases, it's just a time when they mention him. Josh didn't mention him this time, though."

Cormier ultimately questioned the logic behind Jones continuing to pick fights with active competitors when he has seemingly moved on from his own fighting career.

He said, "Jones took the opportunity to try to come at that dude. If you're retired, you don't pick fights with these guys because you're not going to fight them, and you have no intention to fight them. Hokit goes at him, Jon Jones goes at him, so we're talking about Jon Jones when it isn't really his story anymore."

Jones' involvement in the latest controversy is another example of his tendency to enter conversations surrounding other fighters, even when he is no longer directly involved.

From the GOAT debate surrounding Alex Pereira after the White House event to the controversy surrounding Tom Aspinall's eye injury, the former champion has continued to make his presence felt. Cormier's criticism highlights why some fans believe Jones should let the active heavyweight division take center stage rather than repeatedly inserting himself into its rivalries.

#2819. Jensen Huang's — and Nvidia's — legal exposure balloons

The still-unraveling scandal of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's extramarital affairs and the tech giant's potential role in its advanced semiconductor chips ending up in China present a legal minefield for Huang and Nvidia.

Federal and state prosecutors and Nvidia employees may target Huang and the company in court, where some shareholders have already taken action.

That's on top of a rapidly growing pile of probes by Republicans who control Congress now and Democrats who will have majorities in both the House and Senate come January.

"It's not going to get any better for Jensen Huang; it's going to get far worse," said Salvatore Graziano, a New York securities litigation attorney at Bernstein, Litowitz, Berger & Grossmann. His firm is looking at shareholder lawsuit opportunities against Nvidia and Huang.

[Huang] "faces the Department of Justice, the Securities and Exchange Commission and private litigation," Graziano said. "The criminal exposure is very serious. The SEC could bar him from being an officer. And the potential liability [from shareholder] lawsuits can be very large."

Huang's and Nvidia's legal exposure stems from three issues that led up to his shock resignation Nov. 12.

There's his alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, who worked for Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company under investigation for diverting advanced Nvidia semiconductor chips to China in violation of U.S. export rules. That, in turn, has raised questions about whether Huang and Nvidia were aware of any diversions.

There are Huang's two other alleged affairs with Nvidia employees.

And there is the sharp drop in the company's stock price in the wake of the scandal. Nvidia's share price has fallen 19% since Nov. 8, when Huang sent a company-wide apology acknowledging extramarital affairs.

Huang last week retained the law firm Paul, Weiss and its attorneys Melinda Haag and Walter Brown to represent him. Haag is a former U.S. attorney in San Francisco, and Brown is also a former federal prosecutor.

Huang's spokesman at Trident, a crisis communications firm, declined to comment when asked if Haag and Brown are representing him in any potential criminal case and other questions about his legal exposure.

Nvidia did not respond to a request for comment. Last week, Nvidia retained the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an independent investigation into Huang allegedly being targeted by a spy, as U.S. intelligence reportedly has concluded.

The civil liability Huang and Nvidia could face is underscored by a hearing Graziano, the securities attorney, attended Tuesday in U.S. District Court in San Francisco. A judge at the hearing approved a $175 million settlement from Seagate Technology Holdings to resolve a shareholder lawsuit alleging it concealed sales of hard-disk drives to Huawei in China in violation of export bans.

The Seagate settlement came three years after the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security — which is investigating Megaspeed's business with Nvidia — imposed a $300 million penalty on Seagate for its sales to Huawei.

Graziano said his law firm is "actively looking at securities law liability for Nvidia, Jensen Huang and other senior executives in the company given all the recent developments regarding their [potential] knowledge and facilitation of illegal chip sales to Chinese entities."

There is already a pending class-action shareholder lawsuit in San Francisco federal court against Nvidia and Huang by the Rosen Law Firm, for allegedly misleading and false statements related to Megaspeed and Alice Huang.

"Several hundreds of investors have contacted us and expressed an interest to join the case — investors of all walks of life, from mom and pops to investors with very large losses," Rosen attorney Phil Kim told CNBC in an email.

Katie Sweeten, a former federal prosecutor in the DOJ's National Security Division, said the question of "who knew what and when, and what was [Huang's] role" is potentially the biggest risk for Nvidia and him.

"Now DOJ can leverage their criminal authorities," Sweeten, a partner at Scale LLP, told CNBC's Kelly Evans on "The Exchange."

"They can bring grand jury subpoenas, search warrants for emails, bank accounts, personal devices, computers, things like that," Sweeten said.

While it is "not illegal to, you know, sleep with a spy ... it is illegal to pass certain types of information to a counterintelligence officer," she said.

Sweeten also pointed to potential export control violations, which "could be very serious."

She noted the DOJ can indict a company as well as its officials if there is evidence of criminality. Export control violations carry a maximum possible criminal sentence of 20 years in prison and "really large fines," she said.

"You can't send the company to prison, but they can face fines, civil penalties, debarment — where you can't export items down the line," she said.

One example of that came in July 2025, when the DOJ obtained a guilty plea and an agreement to pay more than $140 million from Cadence Design Systems for violating the Export Control Reform Act by unlawfully exporting semiconductor design tools to China's National University of Defense Technology, a school led by China's Central Military Commission.

Another potential legal risk for Huang comes from his planned appearances before Congress.

Huang is scheduled to give a closed-door interview Dec. 1 to three House committees. Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., wants Huang's testimony before that panel to be public, and told CNBC Tuesday he hopes to have that happen "before Christmas."

Sweeten said Huang "has to be very careful" about what he says there because of the risk of being criminally charged with lying to Congress, and of saying something "that absolutely can become part of a criminal or civil investigation."

"I expect Huang to take the Fifth on most questions" from Congress, Jonathan Kanter, a former assistant attorney general in the DOJ's Antitrust Division, told CNBC, meaning Huang will likely use his constitutional right against giving testimony that could be used against him in a criminal case.

"With a Justice Department criminal investigation into Megaspeed already underway and more investigations possible, anything he tells Congress could be used against him," Kanter said. "His lawyers are likely to advise him against taking that risk."

Ben Heineman Jr., a former senior vice president and general counsel of General Electric, and a senior fellow at Harvard University, told CNBC that Nvidia's board will be scrutinized over its actions before it pushed Huang to resign.

Heineman cited the board's apparent lack of succession planning and what looks like weak oversight of Huang, including relating to personal conduct such as the two alleged affairs with Nvidia employees and with an executive of an Nvidia customer.

"When there is a sort of reasonable suspicion, they need to investigate it, and they don't appear to have done that," Heineman said.

Nvidia and Huang could face investigation from state attorneys general, including in California, where the company is headquartered.

On Tuesday, California Attorney General Rob Bonta told Bloomberg Television that the situation at Nvidia with Huang is "something that we're interested in." Bonta's office later told CNBC, "We are monitoring the situation."

Huang's alleged affairs with Nvidia employees also open the possibility of workplace litigation.

Ari Wilkenfeld, a Washington-area employment lawyer, said a consensual relationship between a chief executive and a subordinate "is not in and of itself illegal."

"The liability never comes from the existence of the relationship alone. It comes from whether it was genuinely welcome, what job benefits flowed from it, and what it did to everyone else in the building," Wilkenfeld said. "California law recognizes that so-called sexual favoritism can create a hostile environment for an entire workforce.

"The affair is Huang's problem," Wilkenfeld said. "What, if anything, Nvidia did once it knew is the company's problem."

#2817. How LeBron James dissected Pacers defense down the stretch

How LeBron James dissected Pacers defense down the stretch originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

As he overtook Jason Kidd for the third-most assists in NBA history, LeBron James piloted the Sixers' offense down the stretch against the Indiana Pacers on Sunday.

With Joel Embiid sitting out and Tyrese Maxey getting a breather on the bench, James took over in the fourth quarter. He did so by spamming the same action over and over again.

The set consisted of James getting a ball screen from a guard – either VJ Edgecombe or Anfernee Simons – at the top of the key. After setting or ghosting the screen, the guard would short roll towards the basket.

When the Pacers blitzed or hedged the screen, James found the guard. The Sixers could then attack a 4-on-3 or 3-on-2 situation.

Twice, Edgecombe and Simons scored off the roll.

When the Pacers helped to the roller, that opened up the shooters in each corner.

James directed traffic on offense to ensure that the Sixers spaced the floor properly. Here, he waved Dean Wade out of the dunker spot and into the corner. Edgecombe promptly hit him for an open three off the short roll.

After all of the Sixers' success, the Pacers eventually switched the ball screen. That left the 6-foot-4, 173-pound Quenton Jackson on James, who has five inches and 77 pounds on him.

Jackson and James had already gone head to head on Sunday.

In the first quarter, James set a ball screen for Edgecombe which the Pacers switched. Edgecombe fed James in the post and the Pacers doubled him to support Jackson. James punished them for it.

They didn't double James after the switch in the fourth quarter. This time, James backed Jackson down, backed him down again and backed him down some more before scoring inside.

James' strength abusing matchups against smaller defenders after switches has been a safety valve for the Sixers' offense. Guards struggle to stop him from getting to the basket. When defenses send help, he picks them apart with his otherworldly passing ability.

That combination forced the Pacers to pick their poison. From the 9:55 mark in the fourth until the Pacers switched to a zone defense (after James bullied Jackson) with just over 6:00 left, the Sixers scored 13 points out of this action across seven possessions. That's a stellar 1.86 points per possession.

James, who's scoring a career-low 15.1 points per game so far this season, might not be the elite bucket-getter he was for most of his career. But he's still brilliant at conducting highly efficient offense that pressures a defense's pain points.

#2816. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2814. Congress passes bill to protect older investors from financial fraud

Bipartisan legislation aimed at protecting older Americans and other vulnerable adults from financial fraud has cleared Congress, putting it on track to become law.

Called the Financial Exploitation Prevention Act of 2025 and passed by the House in June, H.R. 2478 was approved by unanimous consent in the Senate Wednesday night — meaning no senators objected to its passage.

The measure will next head to President Donald Trump's desk. The White House did not respond to a CNBC request for comment on whether Trump plans to sign the bill into law.

The bill would generally allow mutual fund companies and their transfer agents, which handle shareholder records and transactions, to temporarily delay paying out redemption requests when they suspect an older or otherwise vulnerable investor is being financially exploited.

"Financial exploitation can have devastating consequences for victims and their families," said Erin Koeppel, a managing director and government relations and public policy counsel at the CFP Board, the organization that oversees the certified financial planner certification. "This legislation fills an important regulatory gap that can help protect vulnerable investors before assets are lost."

The measure's congressional approval comes amid growing financial fraud among older Americans. Those age 60 or older reported losing about $3 billion to fraud in 2025, a 25% increase from $2.4 billion in 2024, according to the Federal Trade Commission's latest report to Congress on fraud among older adults, which was released in October. That amount is 400% higher than the $600 million reported lost in 2020.

"Generally, we support all legislation that aims to prevent any form of financial elder abuse," said Bob Blancato, national coordinator for the Elder Justice Coalition, an advocacy group.

While just 6% of older fraud victims reported losing $100,000 or more in 2025, those losses accounted for 70% of the age group's total reported losses. The number of people reporting losses of that magnitude rose nearly sixfold to 6,769 in 2025, from 1,136 in 2020.

However, because many scams go unreported, the FTC estimates actual losses in 2025 could have been anywhere from $13.3 billion to $119.5 billion. In 2024, the estimated range was from $10.1 billion to $81.5 billion.

Scammers are increasingly targeting Americans' life savings by impersonating banks, businesses and government agencies, according to the FTC. The schemes often involve fabricated emergencies that convince victims to transfer money to supposedly protect their accounts from being compromised.

Additionally, content created by artificial intelligence that can help criminals carry out schemes is becoming increasingly difficult to detect and easier to make, according to the FBI.

Under the Financial Exploitation Prevention Act, mutual fund companies and their transfer agents could temporarily postpone payments when they reasonably believe a redemption request involves financial exploitation.

This would apply to investors age 65 and older, as well as younger adults reasonably believed to have a mental or physical impairment that prevents them from protecting their own interests.

Companies could initially delay paying out redemption proceeds for up to 15 business days. They could extend the hold by another 10 business days if they still reasonably believe exploitation already occurred, is underway or was attempted. State regulators, certain government agencies or courts could also extend the hold.

The bill also addresses the use of a "trusted contact" — an individual whom financial firms can contact when concerns arise about an investor's account. That person may be able to intervene to prevent the fraud by reaching out to the account holder.

Mutual fund companies that opt into the new protections would be required to ask customers to provide the name and contact information of at least one trusted contact.

The legislation would build on existing investor protections. Already, the Financial Industry Regulatory Authority, or FINRA, permits brokerage firms to temporarily place holds on certain transactions if they suspect financial exploitation of an older or otherwise vulnerable investor.

Additionally, FINRA requires brokerages to make a reasonable effort to add a trusted contact to accounts, although investors are not required to provide one. Many banks and other financial institutions also ask account holders for a trusted contact.

Separately, another House-passed bipartisan bill that aims to provide some relief for certain scam victims is awaiting action in the Senate. That measure, the Tax Relief for Fraud Victims Act, is intended to prevent people who lose money to scams from suffering additional financial harm through the federal tax system.

The Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017 temporarily limited deductions for personal theft losses, and Trump's "big beautiful bill" of 2025 made those limits permanent. Some investment scam losses may still be deductible, but losses from some romance and impersonation scams generally aren't, according to a 2025 IRS memo. Victims who withdraw money from a traditional 401(k) or IRA may also owe income tax on the withdrawal and, if they're under 59½, possibly a 10% early-withdrawal penalty.

The Tax Relief for Fraud Victims Act, H.R. 9500, would repeal those deduction limits and waive the 10% penalty on withdrawals tied to deductible fraud losses. The House passed it 408-17 in September, and it was referred to the Senate Finance Committee. It's unclear whether the Senate will take it up before this Congress ends.

"We would hope ... the Senate will join the House in passing H.R. 9500 to end another form of elder financial abuse," Blancato said.

#2813. Observations after Sixers come back for strong shorthanded victory

Observations after Sixers come back for strong shorthanded victory originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stretched their winning streak to four games and snagged a strong road win Wednesday night over the Magic.

They earned a 121-116 victory in Orlando to improve to 13-3 on the season. The Magic dropped to 6-8.

Joel Embiid had 26 points, seven assists, six rebounds, three blocks and two steals.

VJ Edgecombe posted a season-high 24 points, eight boards and six assists.

LeBron James scored 22 points and Anfernee Simons added 18.

Magic star Franz Wagner had 27 points, 11 assists and eight rebounds.

The Sixers had a long injury list in Orlando. Tyrese Maxey (right foot soreness), Jaylen Brown (left hamstring strain) and Dean Wade (left hamstring soreness/tightness) were among their players out. Sixers head coach Nick Nurse told reporters in Orlando that Brown will be re-evaluated when the team returns to Philadelphia.

Orlando was without star forward Paolo Banchero (right high ankle sprain).

The Sixers will start a three-game homestand when they face the Bucks on Saturday afternoon. Here are observations on their win over the Magic:

The Sixers' undermanned starting lineup was Edgecombe, Simons, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, James and Embiid.

After scoring 37 points in the Sixers' win over the Nets, Embiid had a subpar start. He began 1 for 6 from the floor and committed a turnover that led to an Anthony Black dunk.

The Sixers' lineup looked exactly like a unit that hadn't built any chemistry yet. The team was not sharp early on either end of the floor.

Desmond Bane drained a long three-pointer to give Orlando a 27-12 advantage. Orlando made 11 of its first 14 field goals and led by as many as 17 points in the first quarter.

Labaron Philon Jr. and Justin Edwards joined the Sixers' rotation. So did two-way contract players Tacko Fall and Caleb Love.

Philon impressed in the first quarter, converting two floaters and a corner three. The rookie passed his previous NBA scoring high of five points and finished the night with 10 on 4-for-5 shooting.

Fall played 10 minutes and posted two points, three rebounds and three blocks. He slammed in a dunk and rejected two shots early in the second quarter. Credit to Fall for giving the Sixers a good backup center performance and doing his job as a rim-protecting ultra-big man.

Love entered in the second quarter and was ready to fire, hitting a catch-and-shoot three-pointer on his first touch.

The Sixers' zone defense also helped them eat into the Magic's lead. The zone seemed to disrupt Orlando's offensive rhythm and it was active enough to prevent the Magic from consistently creating high-quality looks.

Edgecombe's buzzer-beating mid-range jumper cut the Sixers' deficit to 69-64 after two quarters.

The Sixers pulled within a single point on multiple occasions in the third quarter, including on a Philon three late in the period. They trailed by five points after the third.

Fall continued to play well early in the fourth quarter. He deterred the Magic's drivers from getting all the way to the rim as the very large back line of the Sixers' zone defense and made eye-catching hustle plays on both sides of the ball.

The Sixers outscored Orlando by seven points on the night with Fall on the court. Soon after Embiid subbed back in, he evened the game at 104-all with two free throws. Jabari Walker swished a three to finally push the Sixers in front.

The Magic regained a late lead with a Jalen Suggs three. James then drove and kicked the ball out to Edgecombe, who nailed one of his customary clutch jumpers to put the Sixers back on top.

No matter the night-to-night personnel, the Sixers have been fantastic at the tail end of close games. They're now 11-0 in clutch situations, which the NBA defines as games within a five-point differential in the final five minutes.

#2810. FIA stewards confirm grid penalties for the Las Vegas Grand Prix

#2809. Memphis Grizzlies showing Warriors the new generation

The Golden State Warriors are landing in Memphis tonight caught in a freefall, but context is everything. Steph Curry's Dubs sent Grit and Grind home in 2015 and Ja and Jaren home in 2022. Now Memphis is on its third version, and it's already won at Chase Center. Tonight the Warriors get their second look at these Grizzlies, this time without Steph.

The timing is rough. The Grizzlies are 11-4 and fifth in the West. The Warriors (8-7) limp in on a four-game skid and the second night of a back-to-back, sitting in the last play-in spot. That skid started the night Steph left Houston with an abdominal injury that turned out to be an oblique tear.

Memphis spent the last 18 months taking apart everything it had built. Grit and Grind reached the 2013 conference finals and were up 2-1 on the Warriors in 2015 before Golden State took the next three. The Ja and Jaren teams won 56 games in 2021-22, lost to the Warriors in the second round, and were done after a first-round sweep by OKC in 2025.

Since then, Desmond Bane went to Orlando, Jaren Jackson Jr. went to Utah at the deadline, and in June Ja Morant was shipped to Portland for Jerami Grant and Kris Murray. Coming off a 25-57 season, the Grizzlies added No. 3 pick Cameron Boozer to a young core with Cedric Coward and Zach Edey. Nobody expected much. Then they opened the season with six straight wins, and the rebuild turned into the surprise of the NBA.

The face of the surprise is a familiar one. Ty Jerome, the same guy Steve Kerr called a "godsend" as a two-way player in 2022-23, went from 6.9 points per game in Golden State to leading Memphis at 25.4. In the Nov. 2 overtime loss to New Orleans he dropped 40, including 23 after the third.

Neither team will look like itself tonight. The official report has Curry out with the oblique tear, Jerome out with a right hip impingement, and Draymond Green and Zach Edey out too. That leaves two questions worth watching. Can Brandin Podziemski and Yaxel Lendeborg create enough good shots without Steph and Draymond running the offense? And without Draymond in the paint, can Golden State keep Boozer off the offensive glass?

Steph has seen two versions of the Grizzlies come and go. The third already has a win at Chase. Tonight somebody else has to go into Memphis and get one back.

#2808. Limited practice for Dontayvion Wicks

Philadelphia Eagles WR Dontayvion Wicks (ankle) was a limited participant in practice Thursday, Nov. 19.

Why it Matters: Wicks has been sidelined since Week 6, and he isn't a lock to return this weekend against the Steelers. Even if Wicks does return, it is unlikely he will be more than a WR4 or WR5 given Philadelphia's current quarterback situation.

#2807. Japan core inflation soars to highest this year as energy subsidy expiry pushes up consumer prices

Core inflation in Japan soared to a fresh high in 2026, coming in at 2.2% in October as higher energy costs and their knock-on effects pushed up prices in the wider economy.

Core inflation, which strips out prices of food, was lower than the 2.3% expected by Reuters-polled economists, but was higher than the 2.1% in prior month.

Headline inflation came in at 2.3% compared with September's 2.1%, while the so-called "core-core" inflation, which strips out prices of fresh food and energy, dipped slightly to 2.2% from 2.4% in the month before, government data released on Friday showed.

Energy subsidies for households expired in September, exposing consumers to higher prices brought about by the Iran war. Energy prices climbed 2.2% in October, a sharp spike from the 0.6% decline in September. Gas prices jumped 5.8% compared to the 2.4% rise the month before.

Fuel, light and water charges also saw a considerable acceleration, increasing 3.4% compared to last month's 0.8%.

Following the release, the Nikkei 225 index was down 2.1%, while the 10-year JGB yield fell 2 basis points to 2.805%.

Japan has reportedly approved reintroduction of energy subsidies in December to stave off higher imported energy prices as higher prices crimp growth, hitting consumption and net exports.

The October inflation reading is likely to push the Bank of Japan toward another rate hike in December, despite an unexpected contraction in economic growth in the third quarter. Data from LSEG indicates that there is a 72% chance of a 25 basis point hike from the Bank of Japan.

On Wednesday, BOJ Governor Kazuo Ueda told parliament that the central bank was also "mindful" of the weak yen and rising crude prices, saying that these could hurt ⁠households' real income and weigh on consumption more than expected.

The yen marginally strengthened to trade at 159.59 against the dollar after the data release.

Japan's inflation is likely to still stay above 2% for the rest of 2026 and into the first quarter of 2027, according to Ma Tieying, senior economist at DBS Group Research.

She pointed out that while higher energy prices and yen weakness have contributed to headline inflation, the recent upturn is also supported by domestic factors.

"Wage growth has remained above 3% for nine consecutive months, pointing to a broader pickup in underlying inflation as stronger wage gains feed through to services prices and household spending. This suggests a more durable wage–price cycle is taking hold."

Ma expects the BOJ to raise rates in its December meeting, then twice more in 2027, bringing its terminal rate to 2%.

#2806. One reception for Tyler Warren in Week 11

Indianapolis Colts TE Tyler Warren had one catch for 22 yards versus the Houston Texans in Week 11. He was targeted five times in the loss.

Why It Matters: A breakout fantasy performer from the start in his rookie year, Warren has seen his value fall off a cliff. In his last 16 games, dating back to last season, the Penn State product has topped 50 yards in a game just twice while scoring four total touchdowns (one as a runner). It's been even worse recently with Warren finishing below 25 yards in three of his last four games. Once the Colts traded away their top two healthy receivers there was a glimmer of hope that Warren would emerge as a volume producer, but either he can't get open or Daniel Jones can't find him when he is. Either way, the second-year pro is not someone you can currently trust in your weekly lineup, though retaining him on your bench as a TE2 is a better option that simply cutting him loose.

#2805. Commerce approved sales of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies, Rep. Mast says

The subagency of the Commerce Department that regulates and enforces exports approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies including Alibaba and Tencent as of May 2026, according to documents released Thursday by the House Foreign Affairs Committee.

Some of those approvals, made by Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security, included sales to companies designated by the Department of Defense as "Chinese Military Companies," according to the documents. From December through May, companies had made 3.4 million requests for sales of U.S. AI chips to China, the committee reported, citing those made by Nvidia and Advanced Micro Devices.

"Over the past month, the administration's handling of chip export controls has put our national security and technology edge at risk. The Bureau of Industry and Security has failed to enforce the rules, and Congress must now conduct rigorous oversight of the administration's policies," Rep. Gregory Meeks, D-N.Y., the top Democrat on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said at a meeting of the panel Thursday at which members voted to release the documents.

Spokespersons for Nvidia and AMD said their companies comply with U.S. export control laws.

Nvidia argued it was following the Commerce Department regulations that were in effect at the time.

A spokesperson from the Bureau of Industry and Security said it shared information with Congress on both approvals and denials of licenses for Nvidia's H200 and comparable chips. "Licensing decisions are the product of deliberation and consultation among multiple U.S. Government agencies," the spokesperson said.

"The review and licensing process spanned more than half a year, with approvals subject to strict conditions and limited volumes. Despite receiving some licenses, we have since stopped shipping H200s to China," Nvidia spokesperson John Rizzo told CNBC.

On Tuesday, Nvidia reported $2.4 billion in quarterly revenue from H200 and related chip sales in China. In total, customers based in China and Hong Kong made up about 9.5% of all revenue.

"AMD complies with applicable U.S. export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations," AMD said through a spokesperson.

The approval of 879,000 AI chips is "not that many," Bernstein Senior Analyst Stacy Rasgon told CNBC, adding that this news is not surprising.

"It doesn't sound to me like they're approving everything they got willy nilly," Rasgon said of the Commerce Department. With 879,000 approvals out of 3.4 million requested AI chip sales, he added, "There was a process in place, and it sounds like they approved about one out of every four."

Despite a rapid succession of China export controls on AI chips that started under the Biden administration in 2022, Nvidia and AMD both re-engineered some chips to comply and be sold legally in China. And in December, Trump said Nvidia could ship H200s, its older generation Hopper chips, to "approved customers in China" if the U.S. got a 25% cut of sales.

The U.S. then licensed Nvidia H200 sales to Alibaba, Tencent, ByteDance and other Chinese companies, although sales were limited because Beijing didn't approve large-scale imports, chip analyst Patrick Moorhead of Moor Insights & Strategy told CNBC last week.

H200 sales numbers remained low due to those restrictions on China's end. Nvidia said H200 shipments to China made up less than 1% of data center revenue for the quarter that ended in July.

"In the grand context of the size of this market and how much they're shipping, it's a rounding error. It's tiny," Rasgon told CNBC.

The committee cited the Export Control Reform Act as justification for releasing the data. The panel said the law allows it to declassify and release export data if it "determines that the withholding of the following information is contrary to the national interest."

The license requests released by the Foreign Affairs Committee include requests to sell to major firms including ByteDance, Tesla (Shanghai) and Baidu.

The release of documents comes in the wake of Jensen Huang's resignation from the chip supplier Nvidia after news surfaced that he had several extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy that led to concerns about Nvidia chips being diverted to China.

In the weeks since, Republicans and Democrats in Congress have called for legislation to be passed that would impose stricter export controls on the sale of advanced AI chips to China and other adversaries.

"The license information we are disclosing today will help inform the American public about how BIS has handled licenses for Nvidia H200 and equivalent AMD chips to China. The public has the right to know how these decisions were made," Meeks said.

Mast said two of the firms listed in Nvidia license applications to BIS — Maginfra and New Matrix — are suspected "smuggling fronts." The New York Times reported in November that both companies had empty offices and had ties to companies under national security sanctions.

"Both are fake companies. If BIS approved the sale of these chips to these firms, obviously that is of tremendous concern to us — especially given that President Trump made clear last year that the sale of any AI chips to China will have very real national security conditions," Mast said at the Thursday meeting.

Maginfra and New Matrix did not reply to requests for comment.

Shares of AMD fell Thursday after the investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging the company "has its own China problem." The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Fallout from the Huang scandal on Capitol Hill has intensified in the last week, as lawmakers question the degree to which the former head of Nvidia's relationship with the suspected Chinese spy could have jeopardized national security and American AI dominance.

Huang is due for a closed-door transcribed interview with the House Foreign Affairs panel and two other committees on Dec. 1. Nvidia general counsel Tim Teter is scheduled to appear before the panels Dec. 2.

Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., announced on CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Wednesday that Huang and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick had also agreed to testify.

Lutnick has come under fire over his relationship to Huang and for his failure to take action on Megaspeed, the Singaporean company where Alice Huang, with whom the former Nvidia CEO had an affair, was an executive until 2025. U.S. investigators suspect Megaspeed diverted Nvidia chips to China.

Amid that criticism, the Commerce Department on Monday issued an interim final rule to close a loophole that has allowed Chinese companies to lease Nvidia's advancing computing power from data centers in third countries.

The rule was "written by and at the direction of" the Treasury Department and the White House National Security Council, BIS said in a press release.

— Megan Cassella, Daren Fonda and Kif Leswing contributed to this report.

#2804. Texans DE Chris Braswell seriously injures leg on opening kickoff

Texans defensive end Chris Braswell was seriously injured on the opening kickoff.

Braswell, who was elevated from the practice squad for the game, injured his right leg on what appeared to be a blindside block by Caden Curry. No penalty was called on Seth McGown's 40-yard return.

The team's medical personnel placed his right leg in a vacuum splint before he was carted to the training room.

Braswell's teammates wished him well before he departed on a cart.

It was the first play of the first game Braswell has played this season. A second-round pick of the Bucs in 2024, he had played all 34 games in his first two seasons.

#2803. How the Lakers' Quentin Grimes wants to maintain his hot shooting, even off the bench

When Lakers public address announcer Derrin Horton introduced Quentin Grimes in the starting lineup Wednesday, the easy-going Lakers guard was met with an unusually loud ovation. Dropping a career-high 47 points with 10 made three-pointers less than 24 hours before warranted such a reception.

Grimes has used a recent promotion to the starting lineup to fuel a career scoring binge. But his official place in the starting lineup could be short-lived when Austin Reaves returns from a left heel bruise that's kept him sidelined for three games. No matter if Grimes gets his name announced at the beginning of the game or comes off the bench, he wants to maintain his rediscovered rhythm.

"I feel like we all respect each other's games, we know what everybody can do," Grimes said Wednesday after he scored 20 points in the Lakers' loss to the Utah Jazz. "JJ [Redick] gives everybody credit to go out there, no matter who it is, to be themselves, so I feel like whenever we get back whole I'll still be able to keep playing how I've been playing."

The Lakers ruled Reaves out for Friday's NBA Cup game against the Sacramento Kings at 7 p.m. at Crypto.com Arena. The guard, who was injured during the third quarter against Milwaukee on Nov. 11, did on-court work Wednesday morning before the Lakers' game against the Jazz, and Redick said it went "well" but didn't have any further idea about Reaves' timeline for return.

In the three games without Reaves, Grimes has flourished, averaging 29.7 points, 6.3 rebounds and 2.7 assists while shooting 57.4% from the field and 63.3% from three-point range.

It's not just the "statement" night in which he joined Kobe Bryant as the only Lakers players to make 10 or more three-pointers in a single game. In five starts this season, Grimes is averaging 24.8 points and shooting 55.3% from three compared with 5.9 points and 24% three-point shooting off the bench. The Lakers are 4-1 in games Grimes has started.

Lakers guard Quentin Grimes, shooting over Jazz center Jaxson Hayes, scored 20 points on Wednesday. (Eric Thayer / Los Angeles Times)

Grimes credited his recent success to finding a rhythm after a slow start to the season in which he was eight-for-33 shooting from three in the first 11 games before missing two games because of a sprained right pinky. During his career, Grimes has averaged 14.3 points per start compared with 8.5 points coming off the bench with 59.6% and 57.8% true shooting percentage, respectively.

Grimes has made a career out of shape-shifting. The 25th pick in the 2021 draft once was just trying to crack the New York Knicks rotation as a three-and-D player. With Dallas, where he played briefly with Luka Doncic, and Philadelphia, he had the ball in his hands more often, jumping between the starting lineup and the bench.

Now with two dynamic scorers in Doncic and Reaves, the Lakers are searching for new ways to let Grimes shine.

"A big part is like for him if he's gonna play off-ball, he needs to feel like he's touched the ball and he feels like he's in a rhythm," Redick said. "So, again, this is a new team, and we're feeling out a bunch of stuff for each individual guy. Our job as coaches is try to maximize each player and maximize the group, and there's a learning curve to that."

Grimes, whose relationship with Redick started in 2023 when Grimes visited Redick to work out one summer but accidentally wandered into his future coach's neighbor's house in the Hamptons, has cherished the open line of communication with the coaching staff.

"It's a good problem to have: a coach that wants to get you the ball, wants to get you into a rhythm, stay in that rhythm that I've been in," Grimes said. "So I feel like it's a good thing. He's really good at what he does. We're rolling right now. So I have no doubt he'll be able to keep me in this rhythm once we all get healthy."

Read more: Swanson: One frigid night against the Jazz doesn't change the fact the Lakers are legit

Grimes was thought to be a strong contender for an opening day starting role next to Reaves and Doncic. But the Lakers started wings Matisse Thybulle and Ziaire Williams first along with center Walker Kessler, and then inserted 6-foot-8 forward Jake LaRavia in Williams' place when the Lancaster native missed three games because of a left foot injury.

Redick has said previously he liked the balance of those lineups: Reaves and Doncic for offensive punch, two perimeter, defensive-minded wings to back them up and hit open three-pointers and Kessler to anchor the middle.

The Lakers' lineup with Doncic, Reaves, Thybulle, LaRavia and Kessler still ranks second in net rating among five-man groups that have played more than 50 minutes together.

Instead of focusing on the starting unit, Redick wants to keep tinkering with lineups to find more minutes with neither Doncic nor Reaves on the court. Coaches think those moments could help other players get into the rhythm on their own so that during the second half when the staff is deciding which lineup will finish the game, everyone will be in a good flow.

"I think with this group and the way this group [is], it's just an egoless group," Redick said. "We're going to be able to be flexible with the starting lineup and flexible with matchups."
